Box recognition groups the six normals into three mutually perpendicular antiparallel pairs and derives the frame from them — it deliberately does **not** assume world-axis alignment, and a rotated box is a unit test.
- **`center` follows this codebase's own primitive-op convention, not one invented here**: geometric centre for box/sphere/torus, **base** centre for cylinder/cone — matching what `addBox`/`addSphere` vs `addCylinder`/`addCone` already take, so a candidate could later be handed to those ops without a coordinate-conversion trap. That asymmetry is a documented trap in the roadmap's own Phase 3 notes.
- **`tessellateByGroup`'s trailing free-face group is labelled `solid-N` for an N `collectSolids` never produces** (those faces belong to no solid). The report is keyed by index over the real solids only, so that extra group is simply never looked up.
- **Face ids in the report are LOCAL (`local-N`), not global `face-N`.** `primitiveReport.ts` explores each solid's own handle rather than slicing the global `collectFaces` list: that list is contiguous per solid but discards which solid contributed which, and re-deriving the split by index arithmetic would break silently the first time a free face appeared. A caller wanting a global id uses `inspect`.
- **`fitResidual` has a noise floor, and it is not a defect.** The sampled points come from the tessellation's `Float32Array` buffers, whose precision is relative to COORDINATE MAGNITUDE rather than to the solid's own size, so an exactly-primitive solid far from the origin still reports a small non-zero residual — measured 1.18e-5 for an exact box about 1000 units out, which is right at `1000 × 2^-24`. The first smoke assertion demanded `< 1e-6` and failed for this reason, not because the SDF or the recognition was wrong. Assertions (and callers) compare `fitResidualFrac` or the size of the feature they care about, never zero. Same family as the hidden-line work's finding that Float32 precision tracks coordinate magnitude, which is why that projection centres on the bbox first.
- **A real bug caught by the smoke suite, worth recording as the pattern it belongs to.** Bounding the fillet-search loop to the box's last 12 edges was correct (verified by probe: edges 98–109 all accept the fillet). Also narrowing the *face* scan to the last solid was **not** — a fillet rebuilds the whole shape, so the filleted solid is not necessarily last afterwards, and the assertion silently found nothing. The edge bound was itself necessary: this file's total call volume is close enough to the documented accumulated-heap-pressure threshold that the extra traffic from the sphere/torus assertions tipped a later `render_snapshot` into a WASM abort.

## Decompose imported geometry into parametric primitives — `decompose_to_primitives` (roadmap item 7 Phase 3, closed — *issue #34*)

`decompose_to_primitives` closes issue #34's final phase: for each solid `recognize_primitives` reports as a box/sphere/cylinder/cone/torus, emit creation ops with every dimension bound to a named variable via `exprs` — the repo's **first programmatic producer of expression strings** — plus a parametric script document; optionally writes a brand-new STEP/IGES/BREP file (the export model, like `promote_mesh_to_brep`) and/or saves the script to the macro library. Facts only: unrecognized solids are reported in `perSolid` with a reason, never a guess; the source file is never modified.

- **Export model, not in-place reclassification** — the source stays read-only. In-place would need two genuinely new patterns this codebase has never had (a sidecar that persists geometry and a runtime override of `routeFile()`'s pure extension routing), while the export model reuses `occtService.ts`'s writer pipeline wholesale. The written file is an ordinary B-rep document from the moment it exists; the emitted script (`{variables, steps: [{op}]}` with `exprs` intact) is the parametric artifact. `BREP_ONLY_OPS` gains no new kind.
- **`src/primitiveEmit.ts` (`emitPrimitiveOps`) is the pure, unit-testable emission seam** — like `fitMapping.ts` the only other programmatic op producer — taking `PrimitiveReport` + existing variable names and returning `{variables, ops, perSolid, warnings}`. Box/sphere/torus already use geometric centre, cylinder/cone/torus use base centre — the `Primitive` union was deliberately given those conventions in Phase 2 so no conversion trap remains. Only box needs orientation handling.
- **Box orientation** — candidate carries `xAxis,yAxis,zAxis`. Axis-aligned (each within 1e-6 of a world axis, permutation allowed) → permute `size` to world order and emit a single `addBox` with world-aligned `size`/`exprs` (`box1_x` controls world X). Rotated → emit `addBox` (candidate order, `size[0]` along `xAxis`) plus `rotate {targets:["solid-k"], axisPoint:center, axisDir, angleDeg}` where `k` is the emission index (0-based, empty-base context). `rotationMatrixToAxisAngle` is local pure math (matrix `[xAxis yAxis zAxis]` → axis-angle) with no `THREE` import, deliberately not shared with webview `gizmoTransform.ts`.
- **Variables** — one per dimension, named `{kind}{n}_{field}` (`box1_x`, `cyl2_r`, `cone3_r1`…), `n` 1-based per kind of emitted primitive, collision-renamed against existing document names; value = `Number(v.toPrecision(12))` (12 significant digits, float noise stripped) and `expr` is its string, so file and script agree by construction. Only dimensions are variablized (center/axis stay literal in v1).
- **`src/primitiveWrite.ts` (`buildPrimitivesFile`) is the single new kernel surface** — replays emitted ops over an **empty `TopoDS_Compound`** (`BRep_Builder.MakeCompound`) via `applyEditsBRep`, then `writeShape` at short MEMFS path `/p.{fmt}` with unit scaling/patch, same discipline as `promoteMeshToBrep`. Outcomes that didn't apply become `warnings`; zero ops throws before touching WASM.
- **MCP tool `decompose_to_primitives` (`mcpTools.ts`) wires the 4-touch-point pattern** (`Pipeline` key, `kernelWorker` dispatch, `kernelClient` call). `outputPath`+`targetFormat`+`unit` mirror `promote_mesh_to_brep`'s `DISPLAY_UNITS` fallback + warning + `assertNotSourcePath`; `saveScript` reuses `readScriptLibrary`/`writeScriptLibrary` with the same `overwrite` guard `save_parametric_script` has. Zero recognized → `supported:true` with warnings, no write/save.

Verified via `mcp:smoke`-style live WASM (throwaway scripts, not committed): `examples/STP/block.stp` (3×4×5) → `box1_x=3,y=4,z=5` correctly world-aligned, file bbox/diagonal and mass (60) exact; a box+cylinder fixture both recognized and emitted; `bull.stp` reports `recognized:0`; a cylinder with known radius/height round-trips; the written file re-reads via `load_model`.

## Fitting primitives to a mesh region — `fit_mesh_region` (roadmap item 9, closed — both phases)

Items 7 and 8 both stop at exact geometry: the clip planes derive from a B-rep face or three picked vertices, and primitive recognition reads `BRepAdaptor_Surface` accessors that a triangle soup does not have. This is the mesh-side analogue — grow a region from a seed and least-squares fit a plane/cylinder/sphere, publishing the residual as a fact. `fitMeshRegion` (`src/meshRegionFit.ts`) is the entry point; `src/primitiveFit.ts` is the math and `src/meshRegionGrow.ts` the region walk. Phase 2 (turning an accepted fit into a stored plane or an `addCylinder` op) stays queued, gated on item 7's `plane-N` sidecar.

- **Entirely WASM-free.** No `getOcct` anywhere in the path — the grow and all three fits are pure TypeScript over triangles `parseToWeldedMesh` already produces, so the "opening a pure-mesh file must never load the WASM" invariant holds, as `svgSilhouetteHost.ts`'s mesh path already does. There is also **no triangle-count ceiling**: `meshHeal.ts`'s 50 000 cap exists only because it builds one OCCT face per triangle, which nothing here does (`repair_mesh` set that precedent).
- **`segmentCoplanarFacets` was NOT reused, despite already doing a dihedral-gated flood fill and already being called host-side by `meshioRegionParts.ts`.** Three reasons, each disqualifying on its own: it is **global segmentation with no size cap**, not a seeded grow; it is DOM-free but **not THREE-free** (importing it pulls in `three` + `three-mesh-bvh` and runs a top-level prototype monkey-patch, which every pure mesh module here avoids); and its 15° tolerance is tuned to split **flat faces apart**, whereas fitting a cylinder needs the opposite — a gate loose enough to walk *across* a tessellated curve. The flood-fill discipline is copied from `connectedComponents` (the same walk minus the gate); the default gate is 40°, which still stops at a cube's 90° edges.
- **The dihedral test is `hiddenLineRemoval.ts`'s `normalCos`, now exported rather than copied** — it already normalizes on the fly, returns `NaN` for a degenerate triangle, and **deliberately omits `Math.abs`** so a flipped-winding neighbour reads as ~180° instead of silently passing. `NaN >= cos` is false, so a degenerate neighbour stops the walk, which is the conservative direction.
- **A degenerate SEED is a real case and is now surfaced.** Found while testing: seeding at the pole of a UV-sphere tessellation, where every meridian collapses to one point, yields a zero-area triangle with no normal — so the gate correctly refuses every neighbour and the region stays at one triangle. Correct, but indistinguishable from a legitimate one-triangle surface, so `fitMeshRegion` emits an explanatory warning naming the likely cause.
- **The cylinder axis comes from the covariance of the region's NORMALS, not its points.** Every normal of a cylinder is perpendicular to the axis, so the normals span the perpendicular plane and their least-variance direction IS the axis. Fitting from the points instead gives the direction of greatest extent, which is the axis only for a long thin patch and is wrong for a short wide one. With the axis known the problem drops to a 2D Kasa circle fit on the projected points.
- **A flat region gets NO cylinder candidate, deliberately.** Its normals are all parallel, so their covariance is degenerate and no axis exists — reporting a cylinder of arbitrary radius would be a fabrication. The absent candidate plus a warning is the honest answer.
- **The tie-break is why every candidate is published.** Choosing a single winner by residual alone would let a sphere with a *better* residual beat a perfectly good plane. All candidates ship with their own residuals, sorted plane<cylinder<sphere, and `simplest` applies a **published** threshold (`SIMPLEST_FIT_RESIDUAL_FRAC`) over those same numbers with `simplestRule` stating it — a convenience a caller can recompute or override, never a hidden judgment.
- **`simplestOf(candidates)` is exported and pure, and had to be extracted to be testable at all — found by bug injection, and it corrected a claim this section previously made.** Reversing the simplicity order changed **nothing** in any test, because **no fixture ever produces two sub-threshold candidates at once**. The reason is a genuine correctness property that was being asserted only inside an `if` (so it never ran): a flat region gets **no sphere candidate either**, since the Kasa normal equations are rank-deficient for coplanar points and `fitSphere` correctly refuses rather than returning an arbitrary enormous sphere with a tiny residual. So the earlier "a flat region IS also fitted by a huge sphere" framing was wrong for this code — the ordering is still load-bearing in general, but only hand-built candidates can exercise it. The rule now lives in its own function with direct tests (a better-fitting sphere losing to a good-enough plane; order-independence; `null` rather than the least-bad candidate when nothing fits), which is also what makes `simplestRule`'s "recompute it yourself" claim actually verifiable.
- **A fitted cylinder is INFINITE; `Primitive`'s is not**, so the axis is clipped to the region's own axial extent before the residual is measured. Skip that and every point beyond the caps reads as deviation, and the residual describes the clipping rather than the fit. `primitiveSdf.ts`'s `Primitive` union also gained a `plane` variant for this — the one unbounded member, which B-rep recognition never produces.
- **The residual cannot police that clipping, so the bounds are asserted directly** — also a bug-injection finding. Removing the clip failed no test: a point on a cylinder's lateral surface is at distance **0** from a capped cylinder of *any* height, so an absurd `height: 1e9` still measures a perfect fit. Only asserting `height`/`base` against a fixture of known extent catches it, which a dedicated cylinder-region test now does.
- **Kasa fits minimize algebraic, not geometric, distance**, so they are biased when the points cover only a small cap. That is acceptable *because* the fit is published beside its geometric residual: a biased fit shows up as a large residual rather than a confident wrong answer. Both solve in centroid-local coordinates, which is what keeps a sphere far from the origin accurate (unit-tested at 10 000 units out).
- **No linear algebra existed in this repo before this** — no eigensolver, covariance builder, or linear solver anywhere, and no matrix library in the host dependency set. The 3×3 symmetric Jacobi eigensolver, both Kasa fits and the PCA are all new, hand-rolled to match the dependency-free convention every other pure module here follows.
- **Verified** against analytic ground truth: unit tests recover a known plane normal, a known sphere centre/radius, and a **tilted, off-origin** cylinder axis and radius (an axis-aligned bug cannot pass that one); the grow is pinned at 2 triangles on a cube and 12 once the gate opens past 90°. Live in `mcp:smoke` on committed fixtures with exact ground truth — `cube.stl` (a real 10×10×10 cube) and `large-sphere-100k.stl`, whose radius is exactly 10 at the origin.
- **Five bug injections, each confirmed to land before being trusted**: the largest instead of the smallest eigenvector (4 failures), dropping the dihedral gate (6), skipping the axial clip, reversing the simplicity order, and accepting a candidate with an uncomputable residual. **The middle three initially passed** and are what produced the two findings above — the standard this repo holds injections to is that a *passing* injection means the assertion set is incomplete, not that the code is fine. A restore step also silently failed once (`git checkout` on an untracked new file), contaminating one run; injections now restore from a saved copy.

### Phase 2 — turn a fit into a stored plane / a primitive op (S, gated on item 7’s `plane-N` sidecar, now closed)

- **One pure seam, two consumers.** `src/meshRegionFit.ts` grew `FIT_DERIVED_FROM = "mesh region fit"` (a `derivedFrom` provenance string, alongside `"clip plane"`/`"entered"`), `fitPlaneData(fit)`, `fitOpForKind(fit, kind)`, `fitConstructionPlane(fit, name?)`, and `fitStoreWarning(fit, kind)` — all pure, host-safe and webview-safe, sharing the single place a fitted primitive’s `base`/`axis`/`radius`/`height`/`center` map onto a `ConstructionPlane` or an `AddCylinder`/`AddSphere` `EditOp`. `addCylinder` takes the **base** centre (not the geometric centre), so the fit’s already-clipped `base` is passed through directly, with no conversion trap — `primitiveSdf.ts:37-39` documents this. Both the MCP `store` action and the webview “Save plane” / “Add cylinder” / “Add sphere” buttons call the same helpers; nothing emits an expression string (that would be item 8 Phase 3’s territory), just plain baked numbers validated by `validateEditOp`.
- **MCP: `fit_mesh_region` gains an opt-in `store?: "plane"|"cylinder"|"sphere"` (+ `name` for plane).** Omitted `store` — today’s byte-shape response unchanged (facts only). Named kind is explicit rather than `simplest`-derived, so a flat region fitted by an enormous sphere with a tiny residual is not silently chosen; an absent candidate (a flat region has no cylinder) throws a clear caller-input error rather than degrading. A residual above the published `SIMPLEST_FIT_RESIDUAL_FRAC` still stores, with a facts-style warning quoting both numbers. Plane → `readPlanes` → `nextPlaneId` → `writePlanes` (the exact `set_plane` sequence, whose own tolerant-parse/normalize rule is inherited); cylinder/sphere → `validateEditOp` → `readEdits` → append → `writeEdits(path, newOps, variables)` (the exact `apply_edit_ops` mesh path — persists, the webview replays it as a new body, append-only, undoable, never a silent reclassification of the source mesh). Response gains additive `stored: {kind, plane?, op?}`.
- **Interactive: a “Region fit” sidebar section (`src/webview/regionFitPanel.ts`, DOM-only, unit-test-free per convention, gated to native `stl`/`obj`/`ply`/`gltf` sources via the same `COMPARABLE_MESH_FORMATS` check `meshHealthPanel` already uses).** Eligibility is computed alongside `meshHealthPanel` at the three existing sites (`geometry` → ineligible, `loadUrl` → COMPARABLE check, `loadMeshBytes` → ineligible). A “Pick seed” button arms a one-shot world-point capture (`Viewer.setFitSeedPickHandler`, checked before `measureMode` in `onSelectPointerUp` with the same drag/gizmo guards, reusing `collectMeasureTargets`); the next surface click posts a `fitRegionRequest` with the world-space hit point. A new `fitRegionRequest`/`fitRegionResult`/`fitRegionError` trio (mirroring `meshHeal*`) carries a `requestId` + stale-guard latch identical to `meshHealRequestId`. The host re-reads the document’s bytes (and glTF external buffers via `resolveGltfBuffersFor`) and calls `fitMeshRegion`; the webview renders candidates (kind, key params, residualFrac, warnings) and gates “Save plane” on a plane candidate, “Add cylinder”/“Add sphere” on the matching kind. Saving a plane goes through `PlanesModel.add` → `planesChanged` → host persists sidecar (usable via the Planes panel’s “Use”); adding a primitive goes through `EditsModel.push` → `editsChanged` → host persists. Both are webview-side, so no new button messages were needed.
- **Verification gap, same caveat as every other webview-touching feature:** the actual click → request → render → save/add round trip has NOT been exercised end-to-end in a real Extension Development Host session — see “Verify a change” below. The MCP `store` path is verified headless (see below); the pure mapping helpers are unit-testable.

## Interference / clash detection (roadmap feature)

`doc/roadmap.md`'s "Interference / clash detection between solids or Parts" item (closed) is a natural sibling to `measure`/`measure_exact`/`compare_models` — the overlap volume (if any) between two operands, read-only, via a real `BRepAlgoAPI_Common_3` intersection. Shipped by reusing two already-verified call shapes wholesale, with zero new OCCT probing needed.

- **`entityFacts.ts`'s `checkInterference(extensionPath, bytes, format, ops, a, b)`** takes two `solid-N` id ARRAYS (not single ids, unlike `measure`/`measure_exact`) — `a`/`b` mirror the `boolean` edit op's own operand shape exactly. Multiple ids per operand are compounded via `occtOperations.ts`'s `combineSolids`, **promoted from module-private to exported** specifically for this function (the same "compound the operand's solids together first" framing `booleanSolids` already established internally for the live `boolean` op — this is the first caller outside that file). The intersection is `new oc.BRepAlgoAPI_Common_3(shapeA, shapeB)` → `.IsDone()` → `.Shape()`, identical to the `"intersect"`-kind branch `booleanSolids` already exercises; the resulting volume reuses this file's own private `volumeOf` helper (the same adaptive `BRepGProp` call shape via `src/brepGProp.ts` that `get_mass_properties` uses).
- **An id that doesn't resolve is dropped, never thrown** — reported back in `unresolvedA`/`unresolvedB`. If that leaves either side with zero resolved solids, or the boolean itself doesn't complete, the result is a clean `hasOverlap: false`/`overlapVolume: 0` rather than an error — same graceful-skip convention `booleanSolids` itself already follows on replay for an op whose operands don't resolve.
- **A genuine touching-only pair (shared face/edge/point, zero real volume) also reports `hasOverlap: false`** — gated on `overlapVolume > 1e-9`, not a bare truthiness/`> 0` check, since a floating-point boolean result on an exactly-touching pair doesn't reliably land at literal `0`. **Verified end-to-end against the live WASM** (`npm run mcp:smoke`, a hand-built fixture of 4 boxes): two 10×10×10 boxes offset 3 units apart resolve to an overlap volume of `699.9999999999999` against a `700` analytical expectation (floating-point-exact); a disjoint pair and a face-touching pair both correctly resolve to `hasOverlap: false`.
- **`checkInterferenceTool` (`mcpTools.ts`) is the ONLY place Part-name resolution happens** — `entityFacts.ts`'s `checkInterference` itself stays entirely ignorant of Parts, id-array-in, matching every other `collectSolids`-based function in this codebase (there was no existing precedent for "accept a Part name OR raw ids" anywhere in this codebase before this feature; the closest prior art, `gmshPartsMap.ts`'s per-part tag resolution, resolves to Gmsh tags for physical groups, a different context). Each operand takes EITHER `a`/`b` (raw `solid-N` ids) OR `partA`/`partB` (a Part name, resolved via `readParts()` to that Part's own `volumes` array — `surfaces`/`lines`/`points` are ignored, since interference detection is a solid-only concept). Exactly one of the pair must be given per operand — providing NEITHER is a caller-input-shape error (thrown, matching this codebase's convention of failing fast on unambiguous misuse rather than degrading gracefully, which is reserved for legitimately-unresolvable ids); an unknown Part name, or one with no assigned volumes, degrades to a `warnings` entry and a clean "no overlap" result instead.
- **B-rep sources only headless** (mesh formats need exact B-rep boolean geometry, unavailable without a webview) — same `route.strategy !== "occt"` → `supported: false` gate every other entity-facts tool already uses.
- No interactive-webview counterpart shipped — this is an MCP-only tool for now (the interactive extension has no equivalent panel/button), same "agent-facing tool, no UI required" scope this codebase already accepts for e.g. `run_parametric_script`.

## Standard-parts sourcing: step.parts integration (roadmap feature)

Two MCP tools, `search_standard_parts` and `download_standard_part` (`src/stepPartsService.ts`), over the hosted [step.parts](https://www.step.parts) REST API (`api.step.parts`) — this extension's **first and only external network dependency**. Fasteners, bearings, connectors, extrusions, etc. as ordinary STEP files the existing pipeline already opens — no new format support needed.

- **API shape verified directly against the live service, not assumed from documentation** (same "verify against the real thing" convention this codebase applies to OCCT/Gmsh/meshio++ WASM calls, here applied to an HTTP API instead): fetched the real `GET https://api.step.parts/v1/openapi.json` spec AND ran a real `GET .../v1/parts?q=bolt&pageSize=3` search to confirm field names against an actual response, not just the schema. No authentication required. `/v1/parts` (search: `q` fuzzy text + `tag`/`category`/`family`/`standard` repeatable filters + `page`/`pageSize` pagination) and `/v1/parts/{id}` (single-part detail) are the two endpoints used. `stepUrl` (the STEP file's own download URL, confirmed to live on a **different host**, `media.githubusercontent.com`, than the API itself — `download_standard_part` makes two separate network round trips, detail then file) and `sha256` (confirmed **nullable** — not every part has a recorded checksum) are the fields that matter for downloading.
- **Every function is opt-in by construction — nothing calls this API except the two tools an agent explicitly invokes; there is no automatic/background network activity anywhere in the extension.** This is why no separate settings toggle or enable/disable switch was added: the tool boundary itself already is the opt-in gate the roadmap asked for.
- **Network/API failure is `{available: false, reason}`, never a thrown error** — `stepPartsService.ts`'s `fetchJson()` catches both HTTP non-2xx responses and network/DNS/timeout errors into the same shape (a 10s search timeout, 30s download timeout, via a hand-rolled `AbortController` timeout rather than `AbortSignal.timeout()` for wider Node-version compatibility). `search_standard_parts`/`download_standard_part` (`mcpTools.ts`) surface this as `supported: false` with the reason in `warnings` — **adopted verbatim from step.parts' own error semantics per the roadmap**: this is need-more-info/inconclusive, explicitly NOT "no matching parts" or "part unavailable" (see `describeCapabilities()`'s `verdictConventions` / `headlessLimitations` entries, which call this out by name).
- **Checksum verification is real, not cosmetic** — `downloadStandardPart()` hashes the downloaded bytes with Node's built-in `crypto.createHash ("sha256")` (no new dependency) and compares against the part record's `sha256` (lowercased, matching the API's lowercase hex convention). `verifiedChecksum` is `true` **only** when a `sha256` was present AND matched — never true for a part with no recorded checksum (nothing to verify against is a different state than "verified and passed", and `downloadStandardPartTool` warns distinctly for each: "no recorded sha256" vs. "downloaded bytes do NOT match"). **Re-verified end-to-end against the live API** (`npm run mcp:smoke`): searched real results, downloaded a real part, confirmed a genuine sha256 match — not mocked.
- **Originally no webview/protocol/rendering work at all** — a downloaded part is an ordinary `.step` file on disk; opening it goes through the exact same `routeFile`/OCCT pipeline as any other STEP file. This tool only ever writes a fresh file at the caller-chosen `outputPath`; it never touches any currently-open document's sidecars, so no `assertNotSourcePath`-style guard applies here (there is no "source" being protected against). **This asymmetry (agent-only, no interactive UI) is exactly what the closed "Standard-parts browse and insert panel" roadmap item below fixed.**

## Standard-parts browse and insert panel (roadmap item, closed)

The interactive counterpart of `search_standard_parts`/`download_standard_part` — until now those existed ONLY as MCP tools, so a human user had no way to search step.parts from inside the extension at all. A new **Standard Parts** sidebar section (`src/webview/standardPartsPanel.ts`, added to `viewerDom.ts` alongside Parts/Edits/Variables/FE Mesh/Mass Properties) closes the gap this codebase's own "headless and interactive capabilities stay in sync" convention flags.

- **Reuses `stepPartsService.ts`'s `searchStandardParts`/`downloadStandardPart` directly, host-side — no duplicate HTTP client.** `provider.ts` gained two new message handlers, `standardPartsSearchRequest`/`standardPartsInsertRequest`, following the exact `massPropertiesRequest`-style request/response round-trip convention (webview generates a `requestId`, posts a request, host does the async work and posts back a `...Result`/`...Error` keyed to that same id). A `{available:false}` `ServiceResult` becomes a thrown `Error` inside the handler's `try`, which the existing `catch` converts to the `...Error` message — the SAME graceful-degradation shape `search_standard_parts`/`download_standard_part`'s MCP tools already use, just routed through postMessage instead of a JSON-RPC response.
- **Insert = download + `showSaveDialog` + `vscode.openWith`, not a new edit-op kind.** A part is inserted as a brand-new document (its own tab), not merged into the currently-open model — confirmed deliberate, not a missed opportunity: `editOps.ts`'s ~40 `EditOp` variants have no "import this external STEP as a new solid" kind (every `Add*Op` is a closed-form parametric primitive), and adding one would be real new host+webview engine work, disproportionate for this "S"-sized item. The save dialog defaults to `<part-id>.step`/`.stp` (extension mirrors whichever the part's own `stepUrl` uses) next to the currently open document; dismissing it posts `standardPartsInsertResult` with `path: null` — a quiet no-op (button re-enables, no error toast), not a failure.
- **Deliberately no thumbnails in this pass.** `StandardPart.pngUrl` is a remote `https://` image the main editor webview's CSP has no `img-src` allowance for (unlike `modelComparePanel.ts`'s separate, `enableScripts:false` panel, which added `img-src data:` for its own base64-embedded render snapshots — a different panel with different needs). Fetching thumbnails host-side and piping base64 per search result would work but is real added complexity; a text-only row (name, description, category, standard designation) is the simpler, still-useful v1. Revisit if thumbnails turn out to matter for disambiguating similar-looking fasteners.
- **`StandardPartsPanel` is pure DOM manipulation, unit-test-free like every other sidebar panel class** (`partsPanel.ts`, `meshingPanel.ts`, …) — this project's vitest config has no jsdom/DOM environment, so these classes are verified via manual F5 only, never a headless unit test. The insert-button-per-row state (`Map<partId, HTMLButtonElement>`) is what lets concurrent inserts across different rows each independently re-enable via `onInsertSettled(id)` without a fresh search clobbering an in-flight one — a `standardPartsInsertRequests: Map<requestId, partId>` in `main.ts` is the correlation, mirroring `pending`-map idioms already used elsewhere in this codebase (e.g. `handleExport`'s `PendingExport` map) for the same "more than one of these can be in flight" reason.
- **No new toolbar icon.** The Search/Insert buttons are plain text, not part of the generated `toolbarIcons.ts`/TikZ pipeline — adding a new icon needs a real `pdflatex`+`poppler` pass (see the "Toolbar/panel icons" section above), which wasn't run for this change; a future pass through that pipeline could add matching icons without any other change to this feature.

## Parametric script-as-source: run_parametric_script (roadmap feature)

`run_parametric_script` (`src/parametricScript.ts`'s `compileParametricScript`) closes the roadmap's last exploratory item — a durable, diffable, re-runnable source artifact for from-scratch parts (a bolt circle of N holes costs one script, not N tool calls) — by compiling a small declarative document into ops appended via the **exact same path** `apply_edit_ops` already uses.

- **Deliberately NOT a general-purpose scripting language — this is the answer to all three of the roadmap's open questions at once.** A script is `{variables?, steps}`, where each step is either one `EditOp` (identical to an `apply_edit_ops` entry) or ONE flat `repeat: {times, indexVar, body}` loop (no nesting, no other control flow) whose body ops reference the loop index — and any document/script variable — through the SAME `exprs`/`paramExpr.ts` expression evaluator op fields already use (`sin`/`cos`/`tan` in degrees, `sqrt`, arithmetic — no `eval`, per `paramExpr.ts`'s existing CSP-driven "never reintroduce eval" rule). *Language/engine*: none embedded — no build123d/Python runtime, just this hand-written compiler reusing the existing expression evaluator. *Sandboxing agent-authored code*: moot by construction — there is no general-purpose interpreter, no I/O, no code execution to sandbox; the compiler only ever calls `validateEditOp`/`evalExpr` on data. *Composition with interactive edit-ops*: automatic — a compiled script's output IS an `EditOp[]`, appended to the SAME `<model>.edits.json` op stack manual/ agent `apply_edit_ops` calls already write to, so there is no separate "script mode" the sidecar replay model needs to know about at all.
- **Repeat-generated ops are fully baked, not left parametrically live — a stated v1 scope choice.** Each iteration's body op is resolved against `{...documentVariables, ...scriptVariables, [indexVar]: n}` via the same clone-eval-`setNumericField`-revalidate sequence `editVariables.ts`'s `resolveEditOps` already uses (duplicated here, not called directly, since it also needs to strip `exprs` afterward — `resolveEditOps` keeps it) — then the compiled op's `exprs` annotation is DROPPED entirely. Keeping a loop-index expression alive would be actively wrong on a future replay (no document variable named e.g. `"i"` exists to resolve it against — unlike a real persisted variable, this isn't "stale", it's unresolvable forever). A **plain** (non-repeated) `op` step is untouched by this: it passes straight through `validateEditOp` with `exprs` intact, identical to `apply_edit_ops`'s own behavior — so a value that SHOULD stay live/editable later needs a real document variable (`set_variables`) and a plain op step, not the repeat construct. Script-local `variables` are themselves NEVER persisted — they're compile-time-only convenience, entirely separate from the document's own persisted variable table (a script variable shadows a same-named document variable for that one compile, nothing more).
- **Safety caps, not sandboxing, bound resource use**: `MAX_STEPS=200`, `MAX_REPEAT_TIMES=1000` (per repeat block, clamped, never negative), `MAX_TOTAL_OPS=5000` (across the whole compiled script) — hit any of these and `truncated: true` is returned with the dropped-scope stated in `issues`, never a silent truncation (same "no silent caps" convention as every workflow/agent-facing tool in this codebase).
- **Every malformed step degrades gracefully, per-step, never crashes the whole compile** — same philosophy as `apply_edit_ops`'s per-op accept/reject report: an invalid op, an invalid `repeat.indexVar`, a `repeat.times` expression that fails to evaluate, or a body op whose `exprs` fails against that iteration's values all just get skipped with a reason in `report`/`issues` — the rest of the script still compiles.
- **`run_parametric_script`'s own tool-level behavior mirrors `apply_edit_ops` exactly**: same B-rep-only-op gate for mesh-format sources (`BREP_ONLY_OPS`, same warning phrasing), same `dryRun` (compile + report, no persist), same post-replay entity-inventory response for B-rep sources (topology-changing compiled ops renumber `face-N`/`edge-N` ids, same as any other op). **Re-verified end-to-end against real OCCT** (`npm run mcp:smoke`): a real bolt-circle script (`R*cos(i*360/N)`/ `R*sin(i*360/N)` over 4 iterations) compiled, baked, persisted, and re-tessellated correctly — the first cylinder landed at the expected `(R, 0)` position (angle 0), confirming the trig/loop-index math is correct against live geometry, not just the pure-function unit tests.

## Settings, screenshot, mass properties, and measurement (P1 roadmap features)

Four independent, additive features (`doc/roadmap.md`'s P1 tier): the first `contributes.configuration` settings surface, a view screenshot, a mass- properties readout, and webview-only measurement tools. None touches the read-only-CAD-file invariant.

- **Settings are cross-document *defaults* only, never a per-document override.** `src/viewerDefaults.ts` (`normalizeViewerDefaults`, same clamp-per-field tolerance-gate style as `validateMeshOptions`) reads `workspace.getConfiguration("cadPreview")` in `provider.ts`'s `sendViewerDefaults()`, posted as a `viewerDefaults` message in the `ready` handshake alongside `parts`/`meshingOptions` — arrival order relative to `geometry`/`loadUrl` is **not** deterministic (same discipline as the mesh-size-seed synchronization above), so the webview handler must tolerate either order. `background`/`showGridAndAxes` apply immediately (scene-level); `upAxis` is stored and applied at the next `setModel()` call, rotating the **model root** (`object.rotation.x = -Math.PI/2` for `"z"`) — deliberately NOT `THREE.Object3D.DEFAULT_UP`, a static shared by every `Object3D` including the gizmo/helpers, which would silently affect unrelated code. `resetView()`'s isometric direction is defined in the camera's fixed Y-up world frame and stays meaningful regardless of the setting. `defaultMeshSizePreset` feeds `syncMeshSizeSeed()` via `meshSizeHeuristics.ts`'s `targetSizeForPreset()` (reusing the existing `PRESET_DIVISORS` the Coarse/Medium/Fine buttons already use) — like every other seed input, a persisted per-document `.mesh.json` value always wins.
- **Screenshot reuses the `exportMesh`/`exportResult`/`exportError` + `pending`-map pattern exactly**, just with the format fixed to PNG (no `format` field) — `provider.ts`'s `handleScreenshot()` mirrors `handleExport()`'s mesh-target branch, and the toolbar button (`#screenshot`, posting `screenshotButtonClicked`) and the `cad-preview.screenshot` command (via `EditorSession.screenshot()`) both converge on it, so there is exactly one code path regardless of trigger surface. The webview calls `Viewer.render()` (force a fresh frame) then `captureScreenshotBase64()` (`renderer.domElement.toDataURL("image/png")`) — no persistent `preserveDrawingBuffer: true` renderer flag.
- **Mass properties (`src/massProperties.ts`) is the first `BRepGProp` usage in this codebase — every call shape below is verified against the live WASM**, not assumed from upstream OCCT docs, via the same brute-force overload/arg-count probing convention as everything else in this file: `new oc.GProp_GProps_1()` is the *only* accessible constructor (the unsuffixed `GProp_GProps` has none). Volume/boundary-area integration now goes through **`src/brepGProp.ts`'s adaptive wrappers** (roadmap "Mass properties may be using the under-integrating `BRepGProp` overload", closed): `VolumeProperties2(shape, props, eps, onlyClosed, skipShared)` / `SurfaceProperties2(shape, props, eps, skipShared)` at `MASS_INTEGRATION_EPS = 1e-3`, swapped in uniformly at every volume/surface site across `massProperties.ts`, `modelDiffHost.ts`, `entityFacts.ts`, `meshHeal.ts`, `occtService.ts` and `xcafTree.ts`. The embind-renamed `*2` variants exist because plain-overload names collide on `Standard_Real Eps`; probed live: both work, the 2×3×4 box still integrates to exactly 24 / area 52, and `bull.stp` shows a real +0.015% volume / −0.001% area delta vs the fixed-order `_1` forms (which under-integrate B-spline-trimmed faces — see `brepGProp.ts`'s doc comment for the full numbers). `oc.BRepGProp.LinearProperties(shape, props, skipShared, ?)` is **unsuffixed** but still needs exactly 4 args in this binding, and must only ever be called on a single already-resolved `TopoDS_Edge` — over an entire B-rep shape it double-counts every edge shared by two faces (a 2×3×4 box's `LinearProperties` on the whole shape gave `72`, exactly double the true 36-unit total edge length). `props.CentreOfMass()` returns a `gp_Pnt`-like handle (`.X()/.Y()/.Z()`, needs `.delete()`). `props.MatrixOfInertia()` returns a `gp_Mat`-like handle (`.Value(r, c)`, 1-based, needs `.delete()`) — **verified numerically to be about the CENTROID, not the origin** (the same box gave `Ixx=50`, exactly `(1/12)·24·(3²+4²)`, the standard centroid-based box formula; an origin-based value would have been 200). Volume is only ever computed for the whole model or a `solid-N` (`collectSolids`'s `TopAbs_SOLID` explorer guarantees closure) — never `face-N`/`edge-N` — sidestepping the documented open-shell "plausible-looking but wrong" `VolumeProperties` trap noted near `addVolumeFromSurfaces` above. Entity resolution reuses the **already-shared** `collectSolids`/`collectFaces`/`collectEdges` (`occtOperations.ts`) — the same helpers every edit op uses, so no new id-resolution logic was needed. Mesh sources compute the client-side equivalent (`src/webview/meshMassProperties.ts`, promoted from `meshEdits.test.ts`'s test-only `volumeOf()` signed-tetrahedra helper) with **zero host round trip** — no protocol message is ever sent for a mesh source, verified by the panel never posting `massPropertiesRequest` when `sourceKind === "mesh"`. `computeMeshMassProperties` now also reports `watertight: boolean` by welding the world-space triangle soup (`weldTriangleSoup` + `analyzeMeshTopology`, `freeEdgeCount === 0` — the same pure helpers `checkMeshHealth` already uses), surfaced as a `⚠ Not watertight — volume may not be meaningful.` caveat in `massPropertiesPanel.ts` (`MassPropertiesDisplay.watertight`, `mass-message-warning`) when the computed `volume` is shown (`isClosedTarget` so a single-facet `surface` pick never warns); `main.ts`'s `renderMassProperties`/`setDisplayUnit` thread it through `lastRawMassProperties`/`convertLengthBasedProperties` so a unit change preserves the caveat. `mcpTools.ts`'s `get_mass_properties` follows the same B-rep-only gate, returning `{supported: false}` with a warning for mesh formats (no `OP_PARAM_DOCS` entry needed — that table is `EditOpKind`-only).
- **Measurement is entirely webview-side, display-only, and deliberately independent of `SelectionSet`** — a measurement click must never populate the Parts/Edits working selection, so `MeasurementState` (`src/webview/measurementState.ts`) is its own small 0–2-pick buffer, not `SelectionSet`. `Viewer.setMeasureMode()`/`onMeasurePick` is a **parallel** pick path to `selectionMode`/`onEntityPick`, taking priority for a given click when both happen to be active (`onSelectPointerUp`'s widened guard). Measurement picking uses new unfiltered `picking.ts` helpers (`collectMeasureTargets`/`resolveMeasurePick`) that never "resolve up" to a parent solid, unlike `collectTargets`/`resolvePick` — a face hit stays a face. The raycast intersection's **world-space hit point** (`h.point`) is threaded through for the first time here — the normal `onEntityPick` path only ever forwarded the resolved `{entityType, entityId}` and discarded it. `Viewer.buildMeasurementPick()` also derives, when applicable to the hit entity kind, a world-space direction (face normal via the intersection's local `face.normal` + normal matrix, or edge tangent from the two polyline points straddling the hit — used by the "angle" tool) and the picked edge's full world-space polyline (used by "edgeLength"/"radius" — `measurement.ts`'s `polylineLength`/`circleRadiusFromArcPoints` reuse data already transmitted as `EncodedEdge.positions`, needing no new host work). Client-side triangulated-approximation precision (tied to the existing 0.1 tessellation deflection tolerance) is the **default**, always-instant result for this display-only overlay — an opt-in exact host round trip was later added on top (roadmap "Exact-precision measurement", closed; see the dedicated bullet below), never replacing this fast default. `measurementOverlay.ts`'s canvas label/marker builders **must** follow `geometryBuilder.ts`'s `dotTexture()` lazy-build-on-first-call discipline (no module-scope `document.createElement("canvas")`) — this exact mistake already broke headless tests once, per the Points feature's history above. The overlay's label sprite is rescaled every `animate()` frame (`distance-to-camera × 0.06`) to stay a constant on-screen size while zooming — deliberately different from the point-sprite scale in `frame()`, which only updates on fit/reset, since a label specifically needs to stay legible while continuously zooming, unlike a point marker which only needs to stay visible. The default triangulated-approximation path has no protocol messages and no MCP tool — zero host involvement by design; see the next bullet for the opt-in exact path, which does.
- **Exact-precision measurement (roadmap item, closed)** is a genuine host round trip an agent (or the webview's own `⟳ Exact` button, opt-in on top of the default approximation above) can take for a B-rep source. `src/entityFacts.ts`'s `measureExact(extensionPath, bytes, format, ops, kind, entityIdA, entityIdB?)` handles three kinds:
  - `"distance"` — the true minimum distance between two arbitrary shapes (any point/edge/face/solid combination), via `BRepExtrema_DistShapeShape`. **Verified against the live WASM, not assumed from docs**: only 3 constructor overloads exist in this binding, and calling any of them directly with `(shape1, shape2)` throws an argument-count error (their real params are `(shape1, shape2, extFlag, extAlgo[, deflection])`). Rather than guess the `Extrema_ExtFlag`/`Extrema_ExtAlgo` enum values, the working path is the 0-arg constructor + `.LoadS1(shape1)` → `.LoadS2(shape2)` → `.Perform()` — confirmed end-to-end on a real box-vs-cylinder pair, returning a genuine geometric distance (not a bbox approximation) whose `.PointOnShape1(1)`/`.PointOnShape2(1)` nearest points land exactly where hand-computed geometry predicts.
  - `"edgeLength"` — reuses the exact `BRepGProp.LinearProperties` call shape `getEntityFacts`'s single-edge length already uses.
  - `"radius"` — only valid for an edge whose `BRepAdaptor_Curve_2(edge). GetType()` compares (symbolically, never a hardcoded enum literal) equal to `GeomAbs_CurveType.GeomAbs_Circle`; `.Circle().Radius()` gives the exact value. **Verified end-to-end, not just that the calls don't throw**: a cylinder primitive added via `addCylinder(radius: R, ...)` and re-measured through `apply_edit_ops` → `measure_exact` resolved its rim edges' radius as exactly `R` and its edge length as exactly `2·π·R`. A non-circular edge throws a clear, actionable error rather than a meaningless best-fit number. This is **strictly additive** to `measure`'s existing bbox-centre-to- bbox-centre MCP convention (unchanged, still documented as deliberate — `measure_exact` is a new, separate tool, not a replacement) and to the interactive Measure tool's instant triangulated-approximation default (unchanged). New protocol pair `measureExactRequest`/(`measureExactResult` | `measureExactError`), mirroring the existing `massPropertiesRequest` round-trip pattern exactly (same requestId + stale-response-guard idiom). `main.ts` tracks `lastMeasurement: {tool, picks} | null` (the most recently completed measurement's resolved entity ids) and shows/enables a new `#measure-exact-btn` only when `sourceKind === "brep"`, the tool has an exact counterpart (not `"angle"` — `BRepExtrema_DistShapeShape` has no "angle between two picks" analogue), and the picks actually resolved to real entity ids; reset to `null` on every new model load, tool switch, or Clear, same "stale state" discipline `lastRawMassProperties`/ `explodePreviewBases` already follow elsewhere. `provider.ts`'s handler mirrors `massPropertiesRequest`'s exactly (B-rep-only gate, try/catch → result/error). MCP gets a sibling `measure_exact` tool (`mcpTools.ts`'s `measureExactTool`), same B-rep-only gate as `measure`/ `inspect`, verified end-to-end via `npm run mcp:smoke` (not just unit-tested with a fake pipeline).

## Visualization & UX depth (P2 roadmap features)

Seven additive, mostly-webview-only features (`doc/roadmap.md`'s P2 tier): drag-and-drop open, per-part isolate/hide + Components-tree search, a live exploded-view slider, appearance controls (background/edges/opacity), live clipping/section planes, FE mesh quality statistics, and an orthographic/ perspective camera toggle. Like P1, none of these touch the read-only-CAD-file invariant, and all new state (visibility, clip plane, opacity override, explode preview, background override, projection mode) is session-only — never written to a sidecar.

- **Drag-and-drop's `File.path` branch is very likely DEAD CODE on any current VS Code install — investigated via documented Electron/VS Code version history, not a live F5 session (this environment can't run an interactive Extension Development Host; a real F5 check is still worth doing once, but treat the conclusion below as strong evidence, not "confirmed live").** `main.ts`'s `setupDragAndDrop()` reads `(file as File & {path?: string}).path` off the dropped `File` — a long-standing, NON-standard Electron extension to the DOM `File` API. **Electron 32.0 (released August 2024) removed this property entirely**, not merely deprecated it — `File.path` is `undefined` from that version on; the replacement, `webUtils.getPathForFile()`, is only callable from a preload/Node context, which a VS Code webview's content script (sandboxed, no Node integration) has no access to at all, so there is no in-webview replacement, just a removal. VS Code itself moved to Electron 34 around its February 2025 insiders milestone and has kept pace with upstream Electron since — every VS Code release from roughly mid-2025 onward ships an Electron well past 32, meaning **`file.path` reads `undefined` in the webview of any VS Code version a user is realistically running today** (August 2026). The code's own fallback (`post({type:"openFile"})`, the normal Open dialog) already handles this correctly and needs no change — CLAUDE.md's own prior text already called this out as the acceptable outcome ("if it turns out never to fire, the fallback-only behavior is still correct... only a doc correction here"). **No code change made**: the dead branch is harmless (a single property read that's always `undefined`), costs nothing to keep, and would only matter to remove if this codebase ever needed to drop support for pre-32 Electron VS Code builds, which it doesn't track separately today.
- **Isolate/hide is transient state, deliberately NOT on `Part`/`PartsModel`.** `src/webview/visibilityState.ts`'s `VisibilityState` (hidden-part indices + at-most-one isolated index + hidden tree-group ids) mirrors `SelectionSet`'s "transient, never serialized" precedent, not `PartsModel`'s persisted `Part[]`. `main.ts`'s `applyVisibilityState()` recomputes the full `Viewer.applyPartVisibility()`/`setGroupVisible()` input fresh from this state + `PartsModel.entitiesOf()` on every call (including after every model rebuild), since a freshly built `THREE.Object3D` tree starts fully visible with no memory of prior hide/isolate state. Hiding and isolating **compose** rather than one clobbering the other — clearing isolation never un-hides a part you'd independently hidden.
- **Composing opacity with selection dimming needed a shared baseline, not two writers fighting over `material.opacity`.** `highlightGroup()`'s dim-non-selected behavior and the new Appearance opacity slider both need to scale a material's opacity, and naively writing `mat.opacity` directly from both call sites means whichever runs last wins and silently erases the other's effect. Fixed by tracking `material.userData.baseOpacity` (default `1`, written only by `Viewer.setOpacity()`) and having `highlightGroup()` multiply against it (`mat.opacity = baseOpacity * (dimmed ? factor : 1)`) instead of overwriting — `applyOpacityBaseline()` seeds it on every `setModel()`. Any future feature that dims/fades materials must follow the same multiply-against-`baseOpacity` convention, not a raw assignment.
- **The exploded-view slider previews live by re-deriving from a cached base every frame, never compounding onto the previous frame's result.** `src/webview/explodePreview.ts`'s `captureExplodeBase(root)` snapshots each top-level `userData.groupId`-tagged child's pristine position once (on slider focus/mousedown); every subsequent `applyExplodePreview(bases, factor)` call recomputes each group's position as `basePosition + offsetFromCentre * factor` from that same cached base — the correctness trap a dedicated regression test in `explodePreview.test.ts` covers directly (two consecutive calls with different factors must not compound). It deliberately does **not** reposition a B-rep root's untagged top-level edges/points groups (only `groupId`-tagged solids) — a known, accepted limitation of the live preview only; the *committed* `explode` op still repositions everything correctly since OCCT re-tessellates the whole shape on replay. `editsPanel.ts`'s Apply handler always calls `resetExplodePreview()` immediately before pushing the real op, so the preview transform is never left stacked underneath the authoritative replay; `selectOp()` (switching or collapsing any op form) unconditionally fires the panel's `onExplodePreviewCancel` callback too, so navigating away mid-drag without clicking Apply can't leave an orphaned, unpersisted preview transform on screen.
- **Clipping.** `src/webview/clipping.ts`'s `planeForClip(state, box)` is the single entry point both the clip UI and view-state restore use; it dispatches to `planeForNormal(normal, offsetFrac, box)` (pure `THREE.Plane`/`THREE.Box3` math), with `planeForAxis(axis, …)` kept as a thin wrapper for the three presets. Sweeping `offsetFrac` from `-1` to `1` moves the cut from the box's min-side support plane to its max side, **measured along the active normal** — generalized from the old per-axis `box.min[axis]`/`box.max[axis]` to the AABB support function, `mid = centre·n` and `half = Σ|h·n|`. **For a unit axis normal that reduces BIT-FOR-BIT to the original formula** (`Box3.getCenter`/`getSize` compute `(min+max)*0.5`/`(max-min)*0.5`, the same IEEE ops as `/2`) — verified empirically across 63 cases including a model far from the origin and a sub-millimetre one, not merely reasoned about. That property is what lets `test:webview`'s P1 case assert an explicit `[1,0,0]` renders *pixel-identically* to the `"x"` preset rather than merely closely, and it is why `planeForAxis`'s five original unit tests were left completely untouched — they pin the axis contract through the new general path for free. `Viewer.setClippingPlane()` sets `renderer.localClippingEnabled` and assigns `material.clippingPlanes` across every material on **both** `model` and `meshOverlay` (the FE Mesh overlay explicitly needs the same plane per the roadmap item), and must be re-applied from `setModel()`/`setMeshOverlay()` — `geometryBuilder.ts`'s material factories build fresh materials with no clipping state of their own, so the active plane is cached on `Viewer` and reapplied on every (re)build.
- **The cut face is a real solid cap, not see-through** — `src/webview/ clipCap.ts`, the standard three.js stencil-buffer technique (mirrors the official `webgl_clipping_stencil` example). `MeshStandardMaterial` + `clippingPlanes` alone doesn't paint a cross-section; the cap is built by rendering every clipped mesh's FULL boundary twice into the stencil buffer (back faces increment, front faces decrement, both invisible and ignoring depth) so the accumulated value is nonzero exactly where a point on the cut plane sits inside a solid, then drawing one large quad with a `stencilFunc: NotEqual` test against 0 on top. **Submitted per SOURCE MESH, not merged into one watertight geometry first** — this codebase represents a solid as N separate per-face `THREE.Mesh`es (`geometryBuilder.ts`), not one `BufferGeometry` per solid, but the stencil buffer accumulates additively across draw calls regardless of how many geometries contribute the same triangles, so no merge step is needed (verified: feeding the same solid's boundary in twice — e.g. by accident — is harmless for a `!= 0` test; doubling a nonzero count stays nonzero). **Requires the renderer be created with `{ stencil: true }`** — this three.js version (0.185) defaults it to `false`, unlike older versions; get this wrong and the marking passes silently no-op, degrading gracefully back to the old uncapped look rather than erroring.
- **Structural rebuild vs. cheap plane move — a real perf split, not premature optimization.** `#clip-offset`'s slider fires `setClippingPlane()` on every `input` event during a drag. A full rebuild (dispose+recreate two marker meshes per target mesh, every tick) would be visibly janky for any model with more than a handful of faces. `Viewer.rebuildClipCap()` (full rebuild: new target mesh set) only runs when clipping is toggled on from off, or `model`/`meshOverlay` changes; an axis switch or offset drag takes `updateClipCapPlane()` instead, which mutates the ONE shared `THREE.Plane` instance every marker/cap material's `clippingPlanes` array already references (three.js reads `.normal`/`.constant` at render time, not at assignment time, so an in-place `.copy()` propagates to every material for free) and repositions/rescales the cap mesh via the model+overlay bounding box **cached at the last structural rebuild** (`clipCapBox` — neither an axis switch nor an offset move changes the model's extents, so recalculating it every tick would be wasted `Box3.setFromObject` work).
- **Known, accepted limitation: does NOT reactively track Parts/Components- tree per-entity hide/isolate.** `applyPartVisibility`/`setGroupVisible` (`Viewer`) set `.visible` directly on the affected meshes with no hook into the clip-cap machinery, so a part hidden after the cap was last built keeps showing its cross-section until the next structural rebuild or plane move. The one visibility distinction the cap DOES get right, because it's unavoidable for correctness: `rebuildClipCap()` targets `model`'s `entityType==="surface"` meshes only when `.visible`, and separately targets the FE-mesh overlay's fill mesh only when `this.meshOverlay?. visible` — since `setModelFacesVisible()` already keeps those two in sync (model faces hidden exactly when the overlay is shown), this correctly excludes whichever of the two isn't currently displayed, with no ancestor- visibility walk needed.
- **`clipCap.ts` is now covered by real pixel assertions in `scripts/webview-test/run.mjs` (cases `clip P0`–`P6`)** — it used to have NO test of any kind, with a documented *silent* failure mode (get the stencil wrong and the model just renders uncapped, no error). `capCenterAndSize(plane, box)` (`clipping.ts`) — the pure "where to centre the cap, how big to make it" math — remains separately unit-tested, the same pure/impure split this codebase uses everywhere.
  - **The cap is colour-tagged, not detected by a proxy.** It is a flat quad under a hemisphere + one directional light with no falloff, so every cap pixel is the same RGB; it is painted with `defaultFaceColor()` = `--cad-face`; and **every visible face in the screenshot fixture is Part-assigned** (already established by the palette case), so `--cad-face` reaches the cap and nothing else. Setting it to magenta makes any magenta pixel a cap pixel. Matching is by HUE (`r` and `b` high, `g` far below both), never a fixed RGB, because the cap is LIT — with `#ff00ff` it renders around `(219,31,219)` head-on and `(173,17,173)` at a shallower angle.
  - **The camera must sit on the CLIPPED-AWAY side to see the cut** (`viewDirection = -normal`). three.js keeps the half the normal points into, so viewing along `+normal` shows the surviving half's *outside* with the cut facing away — no cap, and everything looks broken. This cost real debugging: it produced a confident "the clip cap doesn't render at all" conclusion that was entirely wrong. Settled by a standalone three.js scene where flipping the plane's normal is exactly what turns "no cap" into "cap". **The cap was never broken.**
  - **The assertion is a BAND, not a floor, and the ceiling is what catches the documented failure.** Injecting `stencil: false` on the renderer makes the cap paint over *everything* (measured 0.41 of the viewport), which a floor-only assertion would happily pass. Injecting `stencilFunc: Always` does the same.
  - **The tilted case needs its own tighter floor (`TILTED_CAP_FLOOR = 0.02`).** Deleting `repositionClipCap`'s `setFromUnitVectors` leaves the cap at identity orientation — a visibly wrong, misaligned cross-section — yet it still measures 0.0126, clearing the shared `CAP_FLOOR` of 0.008. The correct cap measures 0.024–0.038 across a full offset sweep. Note the axis-aligned case (`P3`) **cannot** catch this at all: identity orientation *is* correct for a +Z normal, so only a tilted normal exercises that line.
  - **Playwright element screenshots include overlapping siblings.** `#view-controls` is a sibling of `#app` painted over it, so revealing the `N` segment produced a reproducible 816-pixel difference that looked exactly like a rendering discrepancy. Any exact pixel comparison must hide that panel first.
  - **Settle time is load-bearing**: posting a `viewState` re-frames the camera, and at 600 ms the captured frame is path-dependent (16k differing pixels for the *same* state); at 1600 ms it is exactly 0. Hence `CLIP_SETTLE`, and hence the `P0` determinism control existing at all — without it a passing `P1` proves nothing and a failing one is unattributable.
- **FE mesh quality statistics use `gmsh.model.mesh.getElementQualities`, confirmed bound and working in the bundled `@loumalouomega/gmsh-wasm` build** (this was open discovery work — not assumed from upstream Gmsh docs). `src/gmshService.ts`'s `computeMeshQuality(gmsh, dimension)` (since renamed to `computeQualityAndWorstElements` when worst-element highlighting was added — same call shape below, still applies): `gmsh.model.mesh.getElements(dim, -1)` returns a **plain object** `{elementTypes, elementTags, nodeTags}` — `elementTags` is an array *per element type*, so every type's tags must be concatenated into one flat array before quality lookup, **not** destructured as a tuple (an early attempt assuming array/tuple shape threw "object is not iterable"). `getElementQualities(tags, "minSICN")` returns `{elementsQuality: number[]}`, one value per tag, in the same order — `"minSICN"` (0=degenerate, 1=ideal) is the metric used; `summarizeQuality()` (`src/meshQuality.ts`, pure/host-side/unit-tested) reduces it to `{min, mean, histogram}`. **Known unreproduced anomaly, handled defensively rather than assumed fixed:** a single early probe run returned an empty quality array against a real generated mesh; 5+ subsequent attempts (including varying whether the tags array was reused vs. freshly copied, and a full `npm run mcp:smoke` pass against complex multi-solid geometry with edits applied) never reproduced it. `computeQualityAndWorstElements()` (`src/gmshService.ts` — the function this stage's `computeMeshQuality()` was later folded into, see below) validates `values.length === tags.length` and returns `{}` (never throws) on any mismatch, so a future recurrence degrades to "no quality summary shown" rather than crashing generation or reporting wrong numbers — `MeshResult.quality` and the `meshingResult` protocol field are both `optional` for exactly this reason. **Separately confirmed while probing this:** GMSH's own default 3D meshing algorithm hangs indefinitely (confirmed via `ps aux` at 27+ minutes / 98% CPU on a trivial box) not only for OCC-*imported* geometry as previously documented above, but for geometry built directly via `gmsh.model.occ.addBox()` too — i.e. the existing `Mesh.Algorithm3D = 4` (Frontal) default this codebase already sets is load-bearing for **any** OCC-kernel-sourced 3D mesh, not just the STEP-import path. Worst-element highlighting in the 3D overlay (colouring the worst-quality tetrahedra) was explicitly scoped OUT of this stage: the 3D overlay only renders **boundary** surface triangles, but bad-quality tets are frequently interior and invisible in that overlay, so a naive implementation would be either misleading or need new, fragile tet-to-boundary-face correlation logic — shipped the quantitative min/mean/histogram summary only, which was the roadmap item's core ask. **This gap was later closed** (see the "Worst-quality-element highlighting" entry near the end of this section) via a different approach than the tet-to-boundary-face correlation floated here — a ghost overlay of the bad elements' own real geometry, not a projection onto the boundary.
- **Orthographic/perspective toggle uses a dual-camera-kept-alive-and-swapped architecture** (`Viewer` gains a `readonly orthoCamera: THREE.OrthographicCamera` alongside the existing `camera: THREE.PerspectiveCamera`, plus an `activeCamera: ViewerCamera` field that everything else — `frame()`, `render()`, `animate()`, the raycaster, `getViewDirection()`/`getCameraUp()` — reads from instead of the old hardcoded `this.camera`), not destroy/recreate-on-toggle, to avoid disrupting `OrbitControls`' internal state and view continuity. `setOrthographic(enabled)` copies position/near/far from the outgoing camera to the incoming one, reassigns `this.controls.object` (three.js supports retargeting `OrbitControls.object` at runtime — `controls` had to be made non-`readonly` for this), and re-`frame()`s in the same view direction. **Use `camera instanceof THREE.OrthographicCamera` to narrow the `ViewerCamera = THREE.PerspectiveCamera | THREE.OrthographicCamera` union — NOT the `.isOrthographicCamera` flag property.** three.js's own type declarations don't expose that flag consistently across the two classes in this project's TypeScript setup (`tsc --noEmit` rejected every `camera.isOrthographicCamera` call site with "property does not exist on type PerspectiveCamera"); `instanceof` narrows correctly and compiles clean. `cameraControls.ts`'s `pan()`/`dolly()` both gained an ortho branch: `pan()`'s per-pixel amount uses the orthographic frustum's `(top-bottom)/ zoom/2` instead of the perspective FOV-tangent distance; `dolly()` under orthographic scales `camera.zoom` (+ `updateProjectionMatrix()`) instead of moving `camera.position`, since moving the camera is a visual no-op under parallel projection.
- **Worst-quality-element highlighting (closed roadmap gap) sidesteps the tet-to-boundary-face correlation problem entirely — the highlight is the bad elements' own real geometry, not a projection onto the mesh boundary.** `computeQualityAndWorstElements()` (`src/gmshService.ts`, the function `computeMeshQuality()` above was folded into) computes the quality summary and, **for a 3D generate only** (a 2D mesh's elements ARE the displayed surface already — no "interior, invisible" problem there), also selects every element scoring below `WORST_ELEMENT_QUALITY_THRESHOLD` (`0.2`, the boundary between the two lowest histogram buckets), sorts worst-first, and caps at `MAX_WORST_ELEMENTS` (`2000` — never a silent truncation: `WorstElementsOverlay.belowThresholdCount` vs `shownCount` reports both). Each kept element's own complete face set is triangulated via the SAME `boundaryTriangles()` (`gmshElementTypes.ts`) the main overlay's boundary uses — but fed ONLY the worst elements as input, so a face shared between two adjacent bad elements dedups away (an interior seam within the highlighted cluster) while a face adjacent to a good (unselected) neighbor stays (the cluster's true outer surface); regression-tested in `gmshService.test.ts` against a fake `GmshApi` (two adjacent bad tets dedup their shared face; a bad tet next to a *good* one does NOT lose that face; a 2002-element pool correctly keeps the worst 2000 and drops the least-bad 2, verified via distinctively-tagged elements at the top/bottom of the sort). **The actual fix for "invisible when interior" is entirely webview-side styling, not host-side projection**: `geometryBuilder.ts`'s `buildWorstElementsHighlight()` renders the selected geometry through a `MeshBasicMaterial` with `transparent: true, depthTest: false, depthWrite: false` — the exact same "ghost" technique the Hidden Lines display mode already uses for occluded edges (see below) — so the highlight paints through occluding faces regardless of true 3D depth, with no clip plane or cutaway needed to see a bad element buried deep inside the model. `Viewer` gains a `worstElementsOverlay` field + `setWorstElementsOverlay()`/ `setWorstElementsOverlayVisible()`, mirroring `meshOverlay`'s dispose/ replace and show/hide-in-place pair exactly, but **deliberately independent of `meshOverlay`'s own lifecycle** — `main.ts`'s wiring calls both explicitly at every site that needs to (a fresh `meshingResult`, the panel's Clear button), never one implicitly clearing the other inside `Viewer`, the same "coupling lives in the wiring layer" precedent `meshingEnabled`'s toggle-sync already established. `applyClippingPlane()` was extended to also traverse `worstElementsOverlay` so the two features compose (a clipped-away fragment is discarded regardless of `depthTest`). The panel gets a `#meshing-worst-toggle` button (reuses the existing `warning` toolbar icon — no new generated-icon pipeline run needed) that **auto-shows itself** whenever a fresh generate has `worstElements` (and auto-hides otherwise) — unlike `#meshing-toggle`, which only ever reflects reality, this one actively surfaces a warning by default, the same framing the large-mesh warning banner already uses; the user can still toggle it off. `MeshingPanel.renderQuality()` gained a `⚠ N elements below quality 0.20 (showing worst M of N)` line under the existing histogram — readout only, rebuilt every `render()`, distinct from the toggle's own persistent on/off state which lives in `main.ts`. See `doc/protocol.md`'s `meshingResult` entry and `doc/webview-api.md`'s `Viewer`/ `geometryBuilder.ts` sections for the full wire format and API surface.

## Camera-aware snapshots and `hit_test` (roadmap Tier 1, closed)

An agent could go from an entity id to a picture, but never from a picture back to an entity id, and `render_snapshot` always rendered the same fixed four views.

- **Everything is opt-in, because the default packet has three silent dependents.** `renderOpsPrefixTool` and `compareModelsTool` both call `renderSnapshot` with `{}` opts; `modelComparePanel` matches results **by label**; `mcp:smoke` asserts exactly 4 images plus the literal label string `A-ISO-A,A-ISO-B,…`. `views`/`composite` are therefore left **`undefined`** unless a caller asks — and `mcpTools.test.ts`'s exact-opts assertion (`{focus, hide, wireframe}`) is the regression proof, deliberately left unedited.
- **`hit_test` is host-side with NO browser — a deliberate departure from the roadmap's "a new message on an existing harness".** `renderSnapshot` already calls `loadBRep` in-process, so the host holds the same triangle soup the webview would raycast. Going through Chromium would have bought nothing and cost a browser launch per call, a `supported: false` path on every installed `.vsix`, and a picking entry point on the interactive `Viewer` that only headless code would use. `silhouetteEdges.ts` is the in-repo precedent for host-side geometry over this same tessellation. It also **upgrades the roadmap's own verification**: "fire a ray down a known axis, assert the face id `inspect` reports" becomes an *unconditional* smoke assertion instead of one guarded by Chromium-absent tolerance.
- **`src/rayPick.ts`** (pure, 18 unit tests) is Möller–Trumbore over faces plus ray/segment closest-approach for edges and points. Faces are **double-sided** (a CAD face may legitimately be hit from behind); edges and points need a tolerance because a zero-width line can never be struck exactly, defaulting to 1% of the bbox diagonal — the same proportional-to-model-size convention the viewer's own `pickThreshold` uses. `volume` mode resolves a face hit up to its owning solid, mirroring `picking.ts`'s `resolvePick`. `hit_test` takes a LIST of rays because parsing and replaying the model dominates the cost and is paid once.
- **A real assertion bug my own smoke test had, caught by the live run**: the first version added a box at the origin and fired a ray down at it — and `hit_test` correctly reported the **bull** at z=20.83, which genuinely was nearer. The picker was right and the test was wrong; the box now sits well clear of the fixture's bbox. Worth recording because a weaker assertion ("some face was hit") would have passed and hidden it.
- **`src/viewDirections.ts` holds the full 14-view vocabulary, and `SVG_VIEWS` is now DERIVED from it — the reason matters.** `SVG_VIEWS`' second importer is **`provider.ts:1696`**, which iterates it to build the Export Silhouette **QuickPick**; growing that table to 14 would have turned a user-facing 8-item menu into a 15-item one. So the curated 7 stay in `svgSilhouette.ts` (same keys, same order) built from the shared table, and drift is impossible by construction. **The ±0.8 Y component in the isometrics is load-bearing** — writing them as `[±1,±1,±1]` would change `SVG_VIEWS.ISO` and therefore every silhouette this repo emits. Lookup is **case-insensitive**: the old lookup was `SVG_VIEWS[name.toUpperCase()]` against uppercase keys while the canonical keys are now lowercase, so without normalizing, an existing `view: "TOP"` would silently fall through to a fallback.
- **`Viewer.framePane`'s camera math was EXTRACTED into `placeCamera`, not parameterized.** `frameBox` needs the same 1.5× margin and ortho/perspective split, but must NOT touch the model-scoped state `framePane` owns — `pickThreshold`, `pointSpriteScale`, grid/axes scale and `lastFitSphere`. Driving those from a zoomed-in subset would shrink the pick threshold and poison the auto-reframe containment gate. Invisible on today's disposable headless page; exactly what would bite if `frameBox` is ever wired interactively, so the split went in now rather than as a follow-up.
- **`screenshot_shape` isolates the entity by default, and that is correctness rather than polish**: a face framed at its own scale usually puts the camera *inside* the parent solid, so an un-isolated shot is the solid's interior or an occluded face. `context: true` opts out, with a warning. It defaults to an isometric because a planar face seen along its own plane is a line. The entity's box comes from `entityBounds()` over the tessellation already in hand — deliberately **not** `getEntityFacts`, which would pay a second full parse and replay.
- **The composite stitches in the BROWSER via one `page.evaluate`**, after the view loop and before `page.close()` — the tiles are already decoded there, so nothing is shipped back over CDP just to be sent out again, and Node has no canvas anyway. It **replaces** the tiles rather than adding a fifth image (a grid costing five images' attention would defeat its own purpose) and draws at half scale into one 1024×768 canvas, so it costs exactly one image. `await img.decode()` inside the evaluate is load-bearing: without it the draws race the decodes and a perfectly clean run returns a blank canvas — the silent-success class this file already documents twice. The smoke assertion therefore parses the PNG's **IHDR dimensions**, not just its signature.
- **Verification**: `viewDirections.test.ts` (16 cases, including that `SVG_VIEWS`' keys, order and values are all unchanged), `rayPick.test.ts` (18), plus live smoke coverage for a named view, an unknown-view fallback, the composite's real pixel dimensions, `screenshot_shape`, and the full `hit_test` set cross-checked against `inspect`.

## Refuse to clobber unsaved buffers, and ship a fix with every refusal (roadmap Tier 1, closed)

Two halves of one idea: don't destroy work, and when you decline something, say how to fix it.

### The guard

- **The MCP server never writes the CAD source** (`assertNotSourcePath`), but both surfaces write the `.edits.json`/`.parts.json`/`.annotations.json`/`.mesh.json` sidecars — ordinary JSON files a user may well have open and hand-edited, and which the extension autosaves on a debounce. A keystroke in the viewer silently clobbered them.
- **The seam is the four store modules, not `provider.ts`.** There are 13+ sidecar write sites in `provider.ts` with no choke point, but `partsStore.ts`/`editsStore.ts`/`annotationsStore.ts`/`meshOptionsStore.ts` are 25–37 lines each with exactly one `writeX(uri, …)` — so `src/dirtyGuard.ts`'s `assertNotDirty()` at the top of each covers **every** call site, including preprocess-restore, for one line apiece.
- **The refusal is visible for free**: every debounced write already had a `.then(undefined, (err) => post({type:"error", …}))` handler, so the thrown `DirtyBufferError`'s message surfaces in the webview unchanged. A silent refusal would be worse than the clobber it prevents — the viewer's state and disk would diverge with no explanation.
- **Guards fail open, never closed.** `hasUnsavedChanges` catches and returns `false`: a broken guard must not also break saving. It compares on **`fsPath`**, not `toString()` — the same file can be addressed by URIs differing in casing or query, and a false negative here costs the user their edits, which is the whole failure being prevented.
- **Scoped to the interactive path, with the headless gap recorded** (the roadmap's own recommendation): `dist/mcp-server.js` is a separate process with no VS Code API and cannot see dirty buffers. A cross-process channel for it was judged disproportionate.
- **Verified in a real VS Code** (`npm run test:integration`, the only harness where `vscode.workspace.textDocuments` is genuinely populated): a dirty sidecar buffer makes the write throw, the file on disk stays **byte-identical**, the message names the file and says what to do, and the write goes through normally once the buffer is clean. Bug-injected by removing the guard — 3 of the 5 checks fail, so the test genuinely discriminates.

### `explainEditOpRejection`

- **The roadmap's premise here was wrong and the design had to change: `validateEditOp` has no reason strings to extend.** It returns `EditOp | null` — a pure boolean gate — across 8 call sites, one of which is the hot sidecar-parse path (hundreds of ops on every document open). Widening its return type would churn all of them for a diagnostic almost nobody reads.
- **So the explainer is a SEPARATE function that runs only on the already-failed path**, called from the two places that report to an agent (`apply_edit_ops`, and `run_parametric_script`/`save_parametric_script` via `enrichScriptRejections`). Zero cost on the happy path, zero blast radius.
- **It lives in `mcpTools.ts`, not `editOps.ts`, so it can quote `OP_PARAM_DOCS`' exact expected shape for the kind** — the most paste-ready fix available. An unknown kind gets the nearest real one by edit distance, but **only for a genuine near-miss** (within a third of the name's length): a confidently wrong suggestion is worse than none.
- `parametricScript.ts` is pure and must not import `mcpTools.ts`, so its generic `"invalid op"` reasons are enriched afterwards from the raw steps, which are still in hand — rather than threading an explainer through the compiler's signature.

## Saved parametric scripts — the macro library (roadmap Tier 1, closed)

Agents re-derived the same bolt-pattern logic every session, and issue #14 asked for the same capability from the interactive side (macros for repeated work). Both land on one mechanism, which is why they were one item.

- **There was no macro language to design, and deliberately none was.** `parametricScript.ts` already compiles a declarative `{variables?, steps}` document with a `repeat` loop and a full expression evaluator, and **`compileParametricScript(raw: unknown, documentValues)` takes untrusted JSON and self-validates** — so a stored script is handed to it verbatim with zero pre-validation. The whole gap was a name and a place to persist it.
- **The one real refactor: `runParametricScriptTool`'s body became `compileAndApplyScript(ctx, modelPath, script, dryRun, extraWarnings)`.** The two tools differ ONLY in where the script document came from; everything else (route gate, `BREP_ONLY_OPS` filter, truncation warning, sidecar write, kernel replay, `maybeRebindParts`, response shape) is script-source independent. So `run_saved_script` has **no second B-rep gate and no second entity-rebinding call site** — verified by the existing 181 `mcpTools.test.ts` cases passing unchanged through the extracted path.
