- **`src/scriptLibrary.ts`** (pure, vscode-free, unit-tested) follows `partsSidecar.ts`'s convention exactly: exported `SCRIPT_LIBRARY_VERSION`, a private file-shape interface, a never-throwing `parseScriptLibraryJson` that drops malformed entries, a `serialize…` ending in a newline. **The stored `script` body is deliberately NOT validated on parse** — `compileParametricScript` is the real, always-current gate, and duplicating its step/op validation here would drift against it. A script whose steps are nonsense still round-trips, so the error surfaces at run time with a real per-step report rather than the macro silently vanishing from the library.
- **A script's own `variables` block IS its parameter list** — no separate parameter schema exists or is needed. `mergeScriptOverrides` merges caller values onto it by name; an override naming an undeclared variable is **reported and not applied**, never fatal. Inventing it would shadow a document variable of the same name for that compile — a surprising action to take on a typo.
- **Storage is one caller-named JSON file, because there is no workspace root to hide it in** — confirmed: every path in `mcpTools.ts` is caller-supplied (`list_workspace_models`' `root` is a *parameter*, and `CAD_PREVIEW_ROOT` points at the WASM bundle, not a user workspace). The interactive panel uses `cad-preview-macros.json` in the model's own **folder** (a macro is reusable by definition, so tying it to one document would defeat the point), which is the same file an agent names as `libraryPath`.
- **`save_parametric_script` refuses to save a script that compiles to no ops** — dry-compiled against its own declared defaults with `{}` document values, since a saved macro must stand on its own rather than depend on some model's variables. A broken macro never enters the library silently to fail later against a real model where the cause is much harder to see.
- **`save_parametric_script`/`list_parametric_scripts` take no `ctx` at all** — the established precedent for kernel-free tools (`setVariables`/`getState`/`listWorkspaceModels` all omit it). Only `run_saved_script` needs one.
- **Interactive: running a macro posts `macroApplyOps` and the WEBVIEW pushes them onto `EditsModel`** — deliberately not a host-side append, so a macro is undoable, visible in the history and removable op-by-op exactly like a hand edit, with no special "macro" state for undo/redo to reason about. **"Record" is a selection over ops already applied**, not a live capture session, which avoids inventing a recording mode and makes it work retroactively.
- **Verified end-to-end against the live kernel** (`npm run mcp:smoke`, mirroring the existing bolt-circle block): the saved macro's defaults produce 4 cylinders, overriding `N` to 6 produces 6, and the `R` override moves the first cylinder to **exactly** the new radius (36.745 → 55.118) — a saved script that ignored its overrides would still "work" and still produce cylinders, so the two-runs-differ-by-exactly-the-parameter check is the assertion that actually proves it. Plus: a script compiling to nothing is refused, ops persist fully baked with no dangling `exprs`, and an unknown name/override degrade as documented.
- **Verification gap, stated plainly**: the Macros panel's click-through (Save current → name prompt → Run with edited parameters → Delete) has NOT been exercised in a real Extension Development Host session. See "Verify a change".

## Hole Wizard — standard tapped/threaded presets (roadmap Tier 1, closed)

- **No new op kind and no kernel surface at all.** `addHole`/`addCounterboreHole`/`addCountersinkHole` already take plain numeric `radius`/`depth`; the gap was that a caller had to already know an M6×1.0 tapped hole wants a 5.0 mm tap drill. `src/holeStandards.ts` (pure, unit-tested) supplies the number; the ops are untouched.
- **Every diameter is in millimetres, imperial designations included** — mm is the unit every edit op consumes, so a table reporting inches would be a trap at the call site. `nominalInch` is carried separately so the designation stays legible.
- **Two diameters per designation, never one recommendation**: `tapDrillDiameter` (hole to be TAPPED) and `clearanceDiameter` (hole a bolt PASSES THROUGH). Which applies depends on intent, and the module does not guess — the same facts-not-verdicts convention `inspect`/`measure` follow.
- **The metric columns follow `D − P` exactly, and a unit test asserts that as an invariant** rather than re-listing every value. This deliberately prefers the exact rule over nearest-shop-drill values (M8×1.25 → 6.75, not 6.8), so the table is internally consistent and a mistyped row is caught mechanically.
- **Interactive: a designation `<select>` fills the EXISTING `radius`/`depth` fields** via `EditsPanel.setNumField` (already public, added for the Transform Gizmo) — no new field type, no new draft shape, and an expression typed into the field afterwards still wins at Apply time. Reuses the existing `.compose-select` class. A tapped pick also sets a 1.5×D blind depth; a clearance pick leaves depth alone, since that depends on the stock rather than the thread.

## Explain the geometry under the cursor (roadmap Tier 1, closed — hover tooltip + inspector card)

The viewer showed shapes but never explained them: clicking a face told you nothing about it, and the `face-N`/`edge-N` ids the whole sidecar model is built on were invisible in the UI. Two of the roadmap item's three pieces shipped here (the selection-groups context menu is the third).

- **The split between the two affordances is dictated by COST, and differs from the roadmap's own framing** (which offered "hovering or selecting" for the card). `getEntityFacts` has **no shape cache** — every call re-reads the source bytes and replays the entire op list — so a hover-driven host round trip would re-parse the model on every mouse move. Therefore: **hover → tooltip (pure webview, instant); selection → inspector card (one host round trip, requestId-latched)**.
- **`getEntityFacts` was ALREADY wired through all four kernel touch points** (`Pipeline` interface, `kernelWorker` dispatch table, `kernelClient`, and `mcpTools`' `inspect`) — the only missing hop was host↔webview. So the card is a new protocol pair (`entityFactsRequest`/`entityFactsResult`/`entityFactsError`, modelled on `massPropertiesRequest`'s per-entity shape rather than `measureExact`'s) over existing kernel surface, with **zero** new kernel work, exactly what the tier's admission permits.
- **`EntityFacts` gained an optional `curveType`** — the edge-side counterpart of `surfaceType`, which had no analogue at all, so an edge card could otherwise only ever show its length. Needed **no new probing**: `BRepAdaptor_Curve_2(edge).GetType()` compared symbolically against `GeomAbs_CurveType` is the exact call `measureExact`'s `"radius"` kind already exercises live in the same file. A new field on an existing function, not new kernel capability — and `inspect` gets it for free. Verified against live OCCT in `mcp:smoke`: the cylinder rim whose exact radius `measure_exact` had just resolved classifies as `"circle"`, a `bull.stp` edge as `"bspline"`, and the two tools **agree** about which edges are circular (an edge `measure_exact` rejects as non-circular must not classify as `"circle"` — a cross-check, not just a smoke-doesn't-throw assertion).
- **`src/webview/entityExplain.ts` (pure, DOM-free, unit-tested) holds the interesting half**: which rows a classification gives meaning to. A planar face gets `Normal`/`On plane`; a cylindrical one gets **neither**, because `EntityFacts` returns `null` for both — this module is where that null becomes an **absent row rather than a blank one**, which is the entire point of the card. A vertex's bbox diagonal is dropped too (always 0).
- **The tooltip says "mentioned by op N", never "used by" / "acts on" — a deliberate honesty constraint, not phrasing.** Entity ids are positional, so the same `face-12` in two ops can denote different topology once an intervening op renumbers (this codebase's own entity-id-drift discussion). A reverse index cannot distinguish the two, so the wording must not claim it can. Pinned by a unit test.
- **`opCatalog.ts`'s new `referencedEntities(op)`** is an exhaustive `switch` with no `default`, deliberately mirroring `describeOp`'s in the same file, covering the eleven operand field names (`targets`, `a`, `b`, `edges`, `faces`, `profile`, `profiles`, `path`, `faceA`, `faceB`, `openingFaces`). **Verified the compile-time guarantee is real rather than assumed** — removing one case makes `tsc` raise TS2366, so a future `EditOpKind` is a build failure, not a silent "no references". (The grep guarding that injection was itself ambiguous — `case "addHelix":` also appears in `describeOpBase` — so the tsc error, not the grep, is what confirmed the patch landed. Same lesson as this session's earlier `sed` injections.)
- **`buildEntityReferenceIndex` is rebuilt in `renderEditsUi()`, not per hover event** — that is the single choke point every op-list change funnels through (sidecar hydration, a user edit, undo/redo/jump, external reconciliation), and `EditsModel.list()` deep-clones the whole list on every call, so recomputing inside a `pointermove` handler would clone the op list on every mouse move.
- **`Viewer.setEntityHoverHandler` is a parallel pick path, not a widening of the click path** — registering one is also what attaches the `pointermove`/`pointerleave` listeners (there was **no `pointermove` on the canvas at all** before this, and no `contextmenu` handler anywhere either). It reuses the click path's exact pane-relative NDC resolution so hover and click can never disagree about what is under the cursor; is suppressed entirely while a drag owns the pointer (`pointerDownPos` set, or `transformControls.dragging`), so it neither fights OrbitControls nor disturbs `onSelectPointerUp`'s 4px drag tolerance; reports only a *change* of entity; and is throttled to roughly one raycast per frame (each raycast re-collects targets).
- **`#hover-tip` must stay `pointer-events: none`** — it tracks the cursor, so without it the tooltip would sit directly under the pointer and swallow the very click it is describing. Regression-tested explicitly.
- **There is NO global `.hidden` rule in `media/viewer.css`** — each consumer defines its own (`.pane-divider.hidden`, `.tb-dropdown.hidden`). Adding `class="hidden"` to the two new elements therefore did **nothing**, and both rendered as empty bordered boxes floating over the viewport. **Every automated test passed anyway**, because they asserted `classList.contains("hidden")` — which was true the entire time the elements were plainly visible. It was caught only by re-running `npm run docs:screenshots` and *looking at* the diff (`viewer-main.png`, `file-menu.png` and `view-controls.png` all churned), the exact "inspect the images, not the exit code" discipline this file already documents. Fixed with explicit `#hover-tip.hidden, #inspector-card.hidden { display: none }`, and the webview tests now assert **rendered** visibility (`offsetParent !== null`) rather than the class — re-verified by injecting the rule's removal, which correctly fails 3 checks that previously passed.
- **A TypeScript trap worth remembering**: `viewerDom.ts`'s markup is one big **template literal**, so a backtick inside an HTML comment terminates the string. Writing `` `getEntityFacts` `` in a comment there produced a bare `TS1005: ';' expected` pointing at an unrelated line.
- **Both the card and the tooltip are cleared on every model rebuild and every new `geometry` load** — the facts describe the pre-edit shape and the ids may have been renumbered, the same stale-state discipline `lastMeasurement`/`lastRawMassProperties`/`explodePreviewBases` already follow.
- **Verified beyond the standing F5 caveat**: `entityExplain.test.ts` (13 cases) and `opCatalog.test.ts` (+8) headless; **six new `npm run test:webview` cases driving the REAL shipped bundle** — hover shows an id and retracts on leave, `#hover-tip` is `pointer-events:none`, a click posts a real `entityFactsRequest` (read back off the harness's own `window.__sent`, which is where its requestId comes from, so the faked reply uses the genuine id), a planar reply renders a `Normal` row and a cylindrical one does **not**, and a reply carrying a **stale requestId is ignored**. The fixture's third op is `translate targets:["solid-0"]`, so hovering in Vol mode also pins the tooltip's 1-based op numbering (`"mentioned by op 3"`) rather than only exercising the empty branch.
- **Still F5-only**: the visual placement/edge-flipping of the tooltip near the viewport border, and how the card reads at a glance.

### The selection-groups context menu (third piece, closed)

- **The reuse the roadmap demanded is exact, and the interesting part is where the ARGUMENT comes from.** "Area ≥ this" is literally `applyFaceFilter(targets, "areaGte", …)` with the **clicked face's own area** as the threshold — the number the query-filter form otherwise makes you type. Same for "Length ≥/≤ this" over `applyLineFilter`. That is the whole discoverability win: identical predicates, no typing, reached by right-click. **No second predicate vocabulary was introduced.**
- **Two groups genuinely could not come from the registry** (`facesWithNormalLike`, `edgesParallelTo` in the new pure `src/webview/selectionGroups.ts`): "same as the one under the cursor" takes a **reference entity**, which `FilterOption`'s `argKind: "none" | "value" | "count"` cannot express. Both are composed from `selectFilters.ts`'s own exported `faceNormal`/`edgeDirection` + `DEFAULT_DIRECTION_TOLERANCE_DEG` rather than added to `FACE_FILTERS`/`LINE_FILTERS`, so they stay inside that module's vocabulary without forking it. **Deliberate asymmetry, pinned by tests**: face matching is sign-SENSITIVE (a box's far side points the other way and is not "the same facing"), edge matching is sign-INSENSITIVE (an edge drawn end-to-start is still parallel — the registry's own `alongX/Y/Z` convention).
- **A group matching only the clicked entity is dropped** — a row reading "(1)" offers nothing a click has not already done. Verified live: on `bull.stp` the menu offers `Same facing 3 / Planar faces 19 / Area ≤ this 36` and correctly OMITS "Area ≥ this", because the clicked face is the largest so that group would have had exactly one member.
- **Volume/point modes return `[]`** — the same gate the filter form applies via `filterSupportsMode` — rendered as an explanatory row ("Selection groups apply to Surf and Line modes."), never a blank menu, which would read as a bug.
- **`Viewer.setContextMenuHandler` calls `preventDefault()` ONLY once an entity actually resolves** — right-click over empty space keeps the browser's own menu, since suppressing it there would remove a capability without offering one. There was no `contextmenu` handler anywhere in this codebase before.
- **The menu lives inside `setupSelectionControls`'s closure** because it needs `selectMode`/`selecting` and the same bulk-inject path `runFilter` uses — the same reason `runFilter` was never lifted out (the existing `globalThis.__syncFilterUi` escape hatch exists precisely because that closure is hard to reach from outside).
- **Hover preview goes through `viewer.renderSelection()` directly, never into the `SelectionSet`** — so moving away restores the real selection by simply re-running `renderHighlight()`, with no bookkeeping to undo. Dismissal is a capture-phase `pointerdown` that `preventDefault()`s, mirroring `dropdownMenu.ts`'s discipline: the click that closes the menu must not also reach the canvas and change the selection.
- **A self-inflicted bug worth recording, same class as this session's `sed` injections**: renaming a local `menu` → `ctxMenu` with a `\bmenu\b` regex also rewrote `getElementById("context-menu")` into `"context-ctxMenu"` — which `tsc` cannot catch, since it is a string. Caught by re-reading the block rather than trusting a clean typecheck. Also fixed in review: the row `pointerleave` listener was being registered inside the open handler, accumulating one per right-click.
- **Verified**: `selectionGroups.test.ts` (9 cases, including that every offered group contains the clicked entity — a group that excluded what you right-clicked would be incoherent) plus **four `npm run test:webview` cases against the real bundle**: the menu is genuinely not rendered before a right-click, every row carries a count, no row advertises a count of 1, Escape dismisses, choosing a group selects **exactly the advertised number** (cross-checked against the status line, so a lying preview would fail), and volume mode shows the explanatory row.

## Theme-reactive scene colours (roadmap Tier 1, closed)

Every 3D scene colour was a hardcoded hex constant tuned for a dark theme — a light-theme user got a `#1e1e1e` void behind light-grey faces. Now driven by `--cad-*` CSS custom properties via `src/webview/palette.ts`.

- **Nothing theme-related existed anywhere before this.** Zero hits across `src/`/`media/`/`scripts/` for `vscode-light`/`data-vscode-theme-kind`/`MutationObserver`/`matchMedia`/`onDidChangeActiveColorTheme`, and `media/viewer.css` defined **zero** custom properties (it only *consumed* 43 `--vscode-*` ones). Both the detection and the palette are new.
- **The real blocker was reachability, not the constants.** `DEFAULT_FACE/EDGE/POINT_COLOR` and `SELECTION_COLOR` already had a re-apply path (`setEntityColors`/`renderSelection`, chained by `main.ts`'s `refreshColors()`), so those were nearly free. What had **no** path: the lights (added straight to the scene with no field holding them — now `hemiLight`/`keyLight` fields), the grid (`GridHelper` bakes both colours into a **vertex buffer**, so a theme change **rebuilds** it — `grid` had to stop being `readonly`), the FE mesh/worst-element overlays, the hidden-line ghosts, and `measurementOverlay.ts`'s marker canvas, which bakes its accent into **pixels** and is therefore memoized keyed on the colour it was drawn with (invalidating a cache, not swapping a material — `geometryBuilder.ts`'s `dotTexture()` needs none of this, being white-filled and tinted per instance).
- **`Viewer.applyTheme()` + `refreshColors()` is the two-step, and the order is load-bearing** — `applyTheme()` covers only the unreachable surfaces above; `refreshColors()` re-themes faces/edges/points/selection through the existing colour path. This is the SAME "a material-affecting change must be followed by `refreshColors()`" contract `setDisplayMode()` already documents, reused rather than reinvented.
- **Per-Part swatches are immune BY CONSTRUCTION, not by a special case** — `setEntityColors` resolves `map.faces.get(id) ?? map.solids.get(groupId) ?? default`, so a themed default is only ever reached in the `else` branch. **Never re-theme by traversing materials and writing `mat.color` directly** — that WOULD overwrite user swatches. The FE mesh overlay needed the same rule made explicit, since its per-part colours live on materials rather than in an `EntityColorMap`: `material.userData.themedDefault` is set `false` for any material built from a real `MeshElementGroup.color`, and `applyTheme()` repaints only the rest.
- **`setBackground` now records `backgroundOverridden`.** Its doc already claimed the "always wins once set" precedent, but nothing tracked it, so a theme change would have clobbered a hand-picked background.
- **Detection is a `MutationObserver` on `<body>`'s `class`/`data-vscode-theme-kind` — no protocol message, no host involvement.** VS Code rewrites both the class and the `--vscode-*` values itself and emits no message for it. The `viewerDefaults` channel was considered and rejected: it is send-once at `ready` with no `onDidChangeConfiguration` listener, so using it would have meant building a re-send path for something the webview can already observe directly.
- **`:root`'s values MUST equal `PALETTE_FALLBACKS`** (the pre-theming constants). Two things depend on it: the default dark theme must render byte-identically, and the screenshot harness sets no body theme class so it resolves against `:root`. Values are explicit literals per theme, **not** `var(--vscode-*)` derivations — the harness defines only 12 of the 43 `--vscode-*` variables `viewer.css` consumes, so keying off an unset one would silently diverge between harness and real session. `.vscode-high-contrast-light` must stay **after** `.vscode-high-contrast`: VS Code can set both classes and they have equal specificity, so source order is what makes the light variant win.
- **Deliberately excluded, with reasons**: `setOpPreview`'s intent tint (lerped destructively at set-time — the pre-tint colour is unrecoverable, and a preview is rebuilt on the next draft change anyway) and `orientationCube.ts`'s R/G/B axis arrows (a cross-tool CAD convention, not chrome). Also unified two stray duplicate literals that should always have been the shared constant (`viewer.ts`'s `meshFromGeometry`, `meshLoaders.ts`) and collapsed `MEASURE_COLOR`/`SELECTION_COLOR` — two constants holding the same value in two files, the second commented "matches the selection highlight" — into one `accent` entry.
- **This item is NOT F5-only, which its roadmap entry could not have known.** Verified three ways: (1) `palette.test.ts` (10 cases) for the parser and the no-DOM degradation — and it caught a real bug, `rgb(-20,0,0)` failing to match at all rather than clamping, because the channel regex lacked `-?`; (2) three new `npm run test:webview` cases driving the REAL shipped bundle — background follows the theme class through dark/light/high-contrast and back, `--cad-face` resolves differently per theme, and a wildly different `--cad-face` leaves a Part-covered render **pixel-identical** while the same mechanism DOES repaint the background (a control, without which the first assertion would also pass if the palette were never read at all); (3) `npm run docs:screenshots` producing **zero** palette churn.
- **A finding worth keeping, from injecting a wrong `--cad-face` into `:root` to prove the screenshot check can fail: it produced no change in `viewer-main.png`.** Not a broken feature — every visible face in the screenshot fixture is Part-assigned, so the default face colour is genuinely never reached there (confirmed by histogram: the model renders in Part swatch colours). That is the immunity property working correctly, and it means **the screenshot suite is structurally blind to `--cad-face`**; re-injecting into `--cad-background` instead repainted 284,790 pixels to exactly the injected value, proving the whole CSS→`refreshPalette`→`paletteColor`→`scene.background`→pixels chain. Hence the dedicated webview-test cases above rather than relying on screenshots for this.
- **A pixel comparison is NOT a material-colour comparison once lights are themed** — a first draft of the Part-swatch test asserted rendered pixels were identical across a full dark→light switch and failed on two colours. Both were genuine Part swatches; they moved because a *shaded* pixel is material colour × lighting, and the light theme legitimately changes `lightGround`. The test now changes only the default face colour, holding lighting fixed.
- **Unrelated fix made in the same pass**: added `vitest.config.ts` excluding `.vscode-test/**`. `@vscode/test-electron` downloads a real VS Code build containing its own `*.test.mts` files, which vitest's default glob picked up and failed on — so running `npm run test:integration` once broke every later `npm test`.

## Display modes (P1 roadmap feature)

Five mutually exclusive whole-model rendering modes (`src/webview/ displayMode.ts`'s `DisplayMode`: `shaded`/`wireframe`/`xray`/`hiddenLines`/ `flat`) replacing the old standalone Wireframe toolbar toggle, driven by a new `#display-mode-group` segmented button group in the view-controls Appearance area. `Viewer.setDisplayMode()`/`applyDisplayMode()` in `src/webview/viewer.ts` is the single entry point; `main.ts`'s wiring is the only caller and always follows it with `refreshColors()` (see below for why).

- **Materials are still built once at load time and only ever have properties mutated — except Flat mode, the one deliberate exception.** Every other mode (shaded/wireframe/xray/hiddenLines) reuses the mesh's original `MeshStandardMaterial`, stashed once as `userData.standardMaterial` at construction time (`geometryBuilder.ts`'s `buildFaceMesh()` and `meshFacets.ts`'s per-facet mesh builder both set it explicitly; `applyDisplayMode()` also lazily captures it from `mesh.material` on its own first run per mesh, as a safety net for the "kept whole" mesh-facet path that doesn't explicitly stash it). Flat needs a genuinely unlit look (`MeshStandardMaterial` always samples scene lights — there is no property to disable that while keeping the class), so it swaps `mesh.material` to a lazily-built, cached `MeshBasicMaterial` (`userData.flatMaterial`) instead. `clearModel()` disposes BOTH cached materials (whichever isn't the currently-active `mesh.material`, already disposed via the normal path) so a mode never used, or used and left behind, doesn't leak.
- **Colours/selection are NOT tracked per-material, so `setDisplayMode()` callers MUST re-apply them afterward.** Because Flat's material is a different instance than the standard one, switching modes can leave the newly-active material at its default colour with no selection highlight. Rather than have `Viewer` maintain two parallel colour/highlight caches, the contract is the same one `setModel()` already relies on for parts/ selection: the caller re-applies. `main.ts`'s `refreshColors()` (already called after every model rebuild/parts change) does exactly this — `setEntityColors()` + `renderHighlight()`/`renderSelection()` — so the Display group's click handler just calls it right after `viewer.setDisplayMode()`. Since `MeshBasicMaterial` has no `.emissive` (unlike `MeshStandardMaterial`, which `renderSelection()`'s face branch used exclusively before this feature), that branch now checks `"emissive" in mat` and falls back to a direct `.color` swap (the same technique edges/points already used for selection) when it's absent.
- **X-Ray's extra translucency is folded into the EXISTING `baseOpacity` composition, not a new opacity writer.** `highlightGroup()`'s formula (already documented above as the one-and-only place `material.opacity` gets written, to avoid the exact "two writers fight over opacity" bug a P2-stage feature already fixed once) gained one more multiplicand: `mat.opacity = base * displayOpacityFactor() * (selected ? 1 : 0.08)`, where `displayOpacityFactor()` returns `0.35` for `"xray"` mode and `1` otherwise. Any future feature that dims/fades face materials must extend this same formula, never write `.opacity` directly.
- **Hidden Lines needs no per-pixel occlusion logic** — it exploits three.js's own opaque-then-transparent render ordering. `buildHiddenLineGhosts()` builds one dimmed `LineBasicMaterial({transparent:true, opacity:0.35, depthTest:false, depthWrite:false})` copy of every edge line, SHARING geometry with the real edge (never disposing it — `clearHiddenLineGhosts()` only disposes materials), grouped into `hiddenLineGhosts: THREE.Group`, a scene sibling of `model` (never a child — same pattern as `meshOverlay`/ `measurementOverlay`), which is why `collectTargets` (`picking.ts`, only ever traverses `this.model`) naturally excludes ghosts from picking with no `raycast` override needed. Because `transparent:true` objects always render in a pass strictly after every opaque object in three.js (faces AND the real, depth-tested edges — bucket membership is decided by `material.transparent`, independent of `renderOrder`), a ghost is guaranteed to paint faintly over the already-fully-rendered frame everywhere its line passes, regardless of true 3D depth — while the real edge, drawn earlier in the opaque pass with normal depth testing, already painted full-strength at every pixel where it's genuinely unoccluded, so it stays visually dominant there (the ghost technically also draws a faint tint on top at those pixels too, a minor accepted cosmetic artifact, not a correctness bug). `applyClippingPlane()` was extended to also traverse `hiddenLineGhosts` so the two features compose correctly together.
- **`setWireframe()`/`this.wireframe` remain a standalone public primitive**, not folded away into `setDisplayMode()`-only internals — `applyDisplayMode()` drives it (`this.wireframe = displayMode === "wireframe"`) for the interactive Display group, but `render_snapshot`'s per-call `wireframe` override (`src/renderService.ts`, `renderViewRequest`'s `wireframe` field) calls `viewer.setWireframe()` directly, bypassing display-mode state entirely — safe because every `renderViewRequest` this feature ever sends targets a disposable, harness-only headless page with no interactive display-mode session to preserve (same invariant the `render_snapshot` section above documents).

## Markup annotation overlay (P1 roadmap feature)

A 2D drawing overlay (freehand/line/arrow/rectangle/circle/eraser, undo/ redo) over the 3D view for review notes, composited into Screenshot exports. Session-only, never persisted — same rule as every other display-only feature (explode preview, clip plane, measurement).

- **Pure-model/DOM split, mirroring `partsSidecar.ts`/`partsStore.ts`**: `src/webview/markupModel.ts` (`MarkupStroke`, `MarkupModel` — push/undo/ redo/clear/list/eraseAt, DOM-free, unit-tested) vs. `src/webview/ markupCanvas.ts` (`drawStroke`/`redrawAll`, DOM-touching, only ever called at runtime, same discipline `geometryBuilder.ts`'s lazily-built `dotTexture()` established — **the actual `<canvas id="markup-canvas">` element itself lives in static `viewerDom.ts` markup, which is fine: a `<canvas>` tag in an HTML template string executes no JS at module load, it's only `document.createElement("canvas")`/`.getContext()` calls at module scope that break headless vitest imports**).
- **The eraser is deliberately NOT part of undo/redo** — `MarkupModel. eraseAt()` removes any stroke with a point within a fixed pixel radius of the cursor immediately and permanently for the session, rather than trying to make an arbitrary (not necessarily most-recent) removal compose with a linear undo/redo stack. `undo()`/`redo()` only ever add/remove the single most-recently-pushed stroke, exactly like `EditsModel`'s op stack.
- **`#markup-canvas` is a scene sibling of the WebGL canvas inside `#app`** (`position:absolute; inset:0`, `z-index` above it), `pointer-events:none` by default so it never intercepts orbit/pick input while markup mode is off — `main.ts`'s `setupMarkupControls()` flips it to `"auto"` for exactly as long as **✎ Markup** is toggled on. Its backing-store resolution is sized in plain CSS pixels (`canvas.width = rect.width`, no devicePixelRatio scaling) — deliberately simpler than matching the WebGL renderer canvas's device-pixel resolution, since `compositeCanvas()`'s `drawImage(overlay, 0, 0, base.width, base.height)` stretch-fits it to the base canvas's size at composite time regardless of any resolution mismatch, at the cost of the interactive on-screen strokes possibly looking very slightly softer on a high-DPI display — an accepted simplicity/quality tradeoff for a review-annotation feature.
- **Composited into Screenshot exports via `Viewer.setMarkupCanvas()` + `src/webview/canvasComposite.ts`'s `compositeCanvas()`** — registered once at webview setup (`main.ts` calls it unconditionally, not just when markup mode is active; an empty/untouched markup canvas composites as a no-op transparent layer, so there's nothing to gate). Both `captureScreenshotBase64()` (the interactive Screenshot feature) and `captureLabeledScreenshotBase64()` (the headless `render_snapshot` MCP tool's per-view capture) composite it in — so an agent calling `render_snapshot` on a document where a human had left markup strokes in an interactive session would see them too, which is correct/expected (`Viewer` has exactly one markup canvas, not a per-caller one).
- **Cleared on every genuinely new model load** (`case "geometry":` for B-rep, `loadMeshObjectFromUrl()` for mesh formats), via a module-level `clearMarkupOverlay` callback `setupMarkupControls()` assigns — strokes aren't geometrically stale (pure screen-space pixels, no model reference at all), but leaving them plastered over a totally different part reads as wrong far more often than useful, so a fresh load resets them like every other session-only display state (explode preview, cached mass properties). Switching *display mode* or applying an *edit* to the SAME document does not clear them — only a genuinely different model does.
- **Live preview while drawing**: `pointermove` calls `redrawAll(canvas, model.list(), previewStroke)` on every event — clears and redraws every committed stroke, then the in-progress stroke on top, so the shape tracks the cursor with no artifacts left behind from the previous frame's preview. Freehand accumulates every sampled point into the live stroke; line/arrow/rectangle/circle keep exactly two points (`[start, current]`) and just overwrite the second one each move.

## Units handling (P3 roadmap feature)

`doc/roadmap.md`'s "Units handling" item (P3 #12) is a **presentation-layer- only** feature — display formatting on top of numbers that are already internally consistent, not a unit-conversion engine.

- **OCCT's STEP reader already auto-converts every shape to one internal cascade unit (millimetres) at read time, regardless of the source file's declared unit — confirmed against the live WASM, not assumed.** Loaded `bull.stp` (declares `INCH` per its `GLOBAL_UNIT_ASSIGNED_CONTEXT`) and `angle1.stp` (declares `MILLIMETRE`) through the exact `STEPControl_Reader_1` → `TransferRoots()` → `OneShape()` path `occtService.ts`'s `readShape()` already uses, then read each result's `Bnd_Box`: `bull.stp`'s bounding box came out `161.29 × 35.04 × 84.04` (mm) — `161.29 / 25.4 ≈ 6.35 in`, a plausible size for the toy-bull model it renders as, confirming OCCT applied the inch→mm conversion during `TransferRoots()` rather than leaving raw inch-scale numbers. **This means there is nothing to convert** — every number this codebase already computes (mass properties, measurement, mesh-size options, edit-op params) is already in one consistent unit. The feature is therefore scoped to (a) surfacing what unit the file was *authored* in, informationally, and (b) a display-unit selector that rescales what a number *looks like* — never what's stored.
- **Detecting a STEP file's declared unit is a plain-text scan, not an OCCT call — this was a deliberate pivot after hitting the usual embind gap.** `UnitsMethods.GetCasCadeLengthUnit()` is bound and callable, but returns the cascade *target* unit (a constant, same for every file) — not what was asked. The natural next step, downcasting `reader.WS().get().Model()` to `StepData_StepModel` and reading its `DynamicType().get().Name()`, hits this build's recurring `"Cannot call ... due to unbound types: PKc"` gap (the same class of binding hole as `Bnd_Box.Get()` and `TopTools_IndexedMapOfShape`, documented above). STEP (ISO-10303-21) is ASCII text, so `src/stepUnits.ts`'s `detectStepLengthUnit()` scans the raw file bytes directly instead: it finds `GLOBAL_UNIT_ASSIGNED_CONTEXT`'s referenced entity ids (the ids that actually govern the shape's coordinate values) and resolves whichever of them is a `LENGTH_UNIT()` complex entity, parsing either its `CONVERSION_BASED_UNIT('INCH', ...)` name or its bare `SI_UNIT(.MILLI., .METRE.)` prefix+base. **Deliberately does NOT just take the first `LENGTH_UNIT()` entity in the file** — verified wrong on `bull.stp`: its first such entity (`#115`) is `SI_UNIT(.CENTI.,.METRE.)`, the *intermediate conversion basis* the file's `INCH` unit (`#121`) is defined against, not the unit actually assigned to the model. Falls back to scanning every `LENGTH_UNIT()` entity (preferring a `CONVERSION_BASED_UNIT` over a bare `SI_UNIT`) when there's no `GLOBAL_UNIT_ASSIGNED_CONTEXT` at all, and returns `undefined` (never throws) for files with no unit declaration whatsoever — confirmed genuinely absent, not a parser miss, in 3 of the ~53 fixtures under `examples/STP/`. **IGES unit detection has since shipped too** (roadmap item, closed) — `src/igesUnits.ts`'s `detectIgesLengthUnit()`, a sibling scanner for IGES's genuinely different, positional (not named-entity) format: IGES is fixed-width 80-column ASCII "card image" text, and the Global section's logical parameter record is split across as many physical `G`-lines as needed (column 73 = `"G"`, columns 1-72 = content), reassembled by concatenating each line's first 72 columns in file order — **trimming trailing whitespace per line first**, confirmed necessary against a REAL OCCT- written file (captured via a live `export_brep` call, embedded byte-exact in `igesUnits.test.ts`): a field that finishes before column 72 gets the rest of the line padded with spaces rather than packing the next field onto the same line, and naively concatenating raw 72-column chunks spliced that padding into the *middle* of the next Hollerith length-prefix token, breaking the parse. A small hand-written tokenizer (`splitIgesParameters`) then splits the reassembled record on `,`/`;`, treating Hollerith strings (`nHtext`) as opaque (their content can itself contain delimiter characters, which must not be treated as separators) — parameter 14 (1-indexed; the unit flag integer) is the target. The 5 flag values this UI's 5 units cover (`1`=inch, `2`=mm, `4`=ft, `6`=m, `10`=cm) map to the SAME canonical name vocabulary (`"MILLIMETRE"`, `"INCH"`, etc.) `detectStepLengthUnit` already returns, so both scanners feed the one shared `src/lengthUnits.ts` mapper — renamed from `displayUnitFromStepName` to `displayUnitFromUnitName` since it's no longer STEP-specific (all call sites updated). Flags not among those five (mile, kilometre, mil, micron, microinch, or `3`/custom-named-in-param-15) degrade to `undefined`, same graceful fallback an unrecognized STEP unit already gets. `provider.ts`'s `handleBRep` now computes `sourceUnit` for both `"step"` and `"iges"` formats (previously STEP-only); BREP still gets none (genuinely no unit metadata in the format at all).
- **The display-unit selector (`#vc-unit`, view-controls' Appearance group) is session-only state in `main.ts`, exactly like every other Stage-2 Appearance control** — never written to a sidecar, reset to the detected unit (or `"mm"`) on every new model load. `src/webview/units.ts`'s `convertLength`/`convertArea`/`convertVolume` scale by the unit's mm factor raised to the 1st/2nd/3rd power respectively; `main.ts` caches the *raw* (mm) Mass Properties result (`lastRawMassProperties`) rather than the already-converted one specifically so that changing the selector can live-rescale an already-displayed result without a new host round trip or mesh recomputation.
- **Moments of inertia are explicitly left unconverted, in both the panel and `units.ts`'s `convertLengthBasedProperties()` — a scope decision, not an oversight.** They're a geometric (not mass-weighted — this codebase has no density concept) moment about the centroid, dimensionally length^5-ish; building a clean unit-suffix story for that on top of the existing ixx/iyy/izz display was judged disproportionate complexity for this stage.
- **The FE Mesh panel's size readout always shows a literal `"mm"` suffix, never `currentDisplayUnit`** (`meshingPanel.ts`'s `refreshSizeReadout()`) — Gmsh's `Mesh.MeshSizeMin`/`MeshSizeMax` options are set and stored in the cascade unit regardless of what the display-unit selector shows elsewhere, so labeling them with anything but `"mm"` would be actively misleading.
- **"Optionally convert units on export" (mentioned in the roadmap) has since shipped for the model Export command, for every B-rep target (BREP/STEP/IGES, via OCCT) and every mesh target (STL/OBJ/PLY/glTF, via Three.js)** — a real geometric scale transform, not a display change; see the "Export" section above for the full write-up, including how STEP and IGES — originally excluded, since neither had a confirmed way to set their own file header's declared unit on write — each got a working, live-WASM-verified fix (a text patch for STEP, a genuinely-working alternate writer constructor for IGES). **The FE Mesh panel's own Gmsh-format export** (`.msh`/Kratos MDPA/VTK/etc.) also gained unit conversion (roadmap item, closed) — see the "Meshing (GMSH-JS)" section for the full write-up (STL vertex scaler, proportional `MeshOptions` rescaling, Export-only scoping).
- **MCP**: `get_mass_properties` (and every other MCP tool) always returns raw cascade-unit (mm) numbers — the display-unit selector is webview-only state with no host or MCP-server counterpart; `doc/mcp-server.md` documents this explicitly so an agent consuming the tool knows what unit it's getting.

## Model comparison (P3 roadmap feature)

`doc/roadmap.md`'s "Model comparison" item (P3 #13) shipped as a B-rep-only, host-only diff **report** — deliberately not a merged 3D scene — which resolves both open design questions the roadmap itself left unanswered.

- **"How do two documents share one custom-editor architecture" — they already do, today, for free.** Investigated before writing any code: `provider.ts`'s `resolveCustomEditor` has no `Map<uri, State>` singleton; every open document gets its own fully-independent closure (`pending` map, debounce timers, `currentEdits`/`currentParts`/`currentMeshOptions`, `session`). `activeSession` is a single provider-instance field, but it's purely a "which tab is currently focused" router for keybindings/Command Palette (set on `onDidChangeViewState`, cleared on `onDidDispose`) — it has no bearing on how many documents can be open. VS Code's Custom Editor API already supports opening the same `viewType` against N different URIs concurrently. **So "view two models side by side" needed zero new code** — open both files in separate tabs and use VS Code's native split-editor UI. What was actually missing was the *diff computation*, which is all this feature adds.
- **"How to avoid misleading false matches" — never collapse a match into a binary verdict; always show the raw numbers behind it.** `src/modelDiff.ts`'s `diffSolids()` (greedy nearest-neighbor bipartite matching by centroid distance, volume as a tie-breaker) attaches `centreDistance`/ `volumeDeltaPct` to every `SolidMatch`, and both `modelComparePanel.ts`'s table and `compare_models`'s JSON surface them directly — a "matched" row with a large displacement or volume delta reads as "heavily edited," not as a false "unchanged," because the confidence numbers are right there instead of hidden behind a computed label.
- **Scoped to a report rather than a merged scene — deliberate, not an oversight.** `Viewer` is hard-wired to one `model: THREE.Object3D | null` (`setModel()` replaces, never adds) — hosting two models in one 3D view with independent visibility/color would be real new `Viewer`/protocol work; a text report was judged sufficient, given side-by-side *visual* comparison already works today via split tabs.
- **Visual diff (roadmap "Visual diff for Compare Models", closed) reopens that "split tabs are enough" call from a different angle — not for the interactive extension (where it's still true) but for the MCP tool, which has no tabs at all.** An agent calling `compare_models` gets only the numeric diff unless it explicitly asks for more; embedding actual before/after PNGs closes that gap without touching `Viewer`/the merged-scene question at all — it reuses `render_snapshot`'s already-shipped headless engine (`src/renderService.ts`) as a black box, one call per B-rep side, same `DEFAULT_VIEWS` four-view packet `render_snapshot` itself defaults to.
  - **`modelComparePanel.ts`'s `renderCompareSnapshots()`** probes `isRenderAvailable()` **once** (not once per side) and only when at least one side is a B-rep source, then renders both B-rep sides in `Promise.all` (verified safe — `renderSnapshot()` has no shared/module-level state, each call gets its own disposable Chromium instance and ephemeral-port HTTP server, see `renderService.ts`'s own doc comment). A mesh-format (STL/OBJ/PLY) side, or an unavailable renderer, degrades to a `warnings` entry — the numeric diff is never blocked or delayed by a failed/skipped snapshot. The panel's own CSP (`default-src 'none'; style-src 'unsafe-inline'`) had no `img-src` at all — silently blocking every `<img>` — so it gained `img-src data:` (inline base64 only; no `localResourceRoots`/`asWebviewUri` needed, consistent with `enableScripts: false`). Images render as a view-direction × side grid (`DEFAULT_VIEWS` order down the rows, A/B across the columns) so a difference is easy to spot from whichever angle shows it, rather than one thumbnail per side.
  - **`compareModelsTool`'s new `includeSnapshots` param defaults to `false` — opt-in, not automatic.** Unlike the interactive panel (a deliberate one-shot manual command where a few extra seconds of rendering is an acceptable cost every time), an MCP call could be far more frequent/automated, and each image is real token cost in the agent's context an agent should choose to pay, not have imposed on every `compare_models` call. When set, mirrors the panel's exact mechanism (`ctx.pipeline.isRenderAvailable()` once, `ctx.pipeline.renderSnapshot()` per B-rep side in parallel) and prefixes each returned `RenderImage.label` with `"A-"`/`"B-"` (e.g. `"A-ISO-A"`) so an agent can tell which side an image belongs to without cross-referencing order. **No `mcpServer.ts`/`wrap()` changes were needed at all** — the generic `images?: RenderImage[]` → image-content-block mechanism `render_snapshot` already established (see `wrap()`'s `hasImages()` check) picks up `compare_models`'s new `images` field automatically; `compare_models` is simply the second tool to use it. Verified end-to-end via `npm run mcp:smoke`: `includeSnapshots` omitted returns no `images` key at all (not even `[]`); a B-rep-vs-B-rep comparison with `includeSnapshots: true` returns exactly 8 labelled images (4 per side) and 8 raw image content blocks, when Playwright/Chromium are available in the running environment — the same environment-dependent tolerance `render_snapshot`'s own smoke coverage already requires (this repo's own dev environment happens to run Node 18, below Playwright's minimum, so the smoke script's `supported: false` branch — not the image-bearing one — is what's actually exercised there; both branches are asserted).
  - **`compare_models`'s MCP tool description/schema were also corrected while in the area — a pre-existing, unrelated drift, not introduced by this feature.** The registered tool description in `mcpServer.ts` still said "Diff two B-rep models... B-rep sources only headless (mesh formats return supported: false)", stale since STL/OBJ/PLY support shipped; fixed to match `describeCapabilities()`'s and `doc/mcp-server.md`'s already-accurate text.
- **STL support (roadmap "Mesh-source model comparison", closed) — a new host-side STL parser, not a webview round trip.** Originally B-rep only: mesh formats had no OCCT shape for the host to independently query, and "there is no host-side mesh parser anywhere in this codebase" was a standing, explicitly documented limitation (`compare_models`'s own error message said so verbatim). Closed by writing one, scoped to STL specifically (not OBJ/PLY/glTF at the time) — STL is this codebase's own lowest-common-denominator mesh interchange format already (`meshioService.ts` funnels every meshio-only format through it), and its flat, unindexed triangle-soup structure is by far the simplest of the four to parse from scratch. Three new pure (vscode/OCCT/THREE-free), independently unit-tested modules: `src/stlParser.ts` (`parseStl()` — binary STL detected by exact expected-size match against the header's declared triangle count, `84 + count*50`, NOT by sniffing the header text for `"solid"`, the classic trap: a binary file's free-form 80-byte header may itself start with that word); `src/meshComponents.ts` (`weldTriangleSoup()` — quantized-position hash dedup, the same technique `meshFacets.ts`'s `canonOf` already uses for the identical "STL has no native shared-vertex indexing" problem; `connectedComponents()` — edge-adjacency-map + BFS flood-fill with NO angle gate, i.e. `meshFacets.ts`'s `segmentCoplanarFacets` scaffolding solving the *opposite* problem: that one merges triangles into flat FACES by gating on face-normal angle, this one merges triangles into whole SOLIDS with no gate at all; `boundsOfTriangles`/`boundsCenter`/ `boundsDiagonal`; `volumeOfTriangles` — signed-tetrahedra-vs-origin sum, the same formula `gmshElementTypes.ts`'s `signedVolume` and the webview's `meshMassProperties.ts` already use elsewhere in this codebase, verified against a unit box (volume 1) and the *same* 2×3×4 box CLAUDE.md's B-rep `BRepGProp` verification uses (volume 24 — a deliberate cross-check that both engines agree)); `src/stlSolidSignatures.ts` (`extractStlSolidSignatures()` — wires the above into the `SolidSignature[]` shape `modelDiff.ts` needs, one signature per connected component, ids `solid-0`/`solid-1`/… by first-encountered-triangle order). **`modelDiffHost.ts`'s `compareModels()` was generalized to a `CompareSource` discriminated union** instead of positional B-rep-only params, dispatching per-side by `kind`.
- **OBJ/PLY support (roadmap item, closed) — two more host-side parsers, reusing `meshComponents.ts` unchanged; glTF remains the one genuinely out-of-reach format, and deliberately so.** Both new parsers follow the exact same pure/vscode/OCCT/THREE-free convention `stlParser.ts` established, and both were tractable to hand-roll correctly and test thoroughly (including real binary-format round-tripping for PLY) without needing a full third-party mesh-loading engine:
  - **`src/objParser.ts`** (`parseObj()`) — OBJ is plain ASCII text with ALREADY-shared vertex indices (`f` lines reference `v` lines by index), so unlike STL it parses directly into an indexed mesh with **no `weldTriangleSoup()` pass needed at all** — this is the one structural difference from the STL path. Resolves `v`/`v/vt`/`v/vt/vn`/`v//vn` face-vertex reference forms (only the leading `v` index is ever used); 1-based positive indices and OBJ's negative *relative-to-current-vertex- count* indices are both handled (verified with a fixture using `-3 -2 -1` against 3 known vertices). A face with more than 3 vertices is fan-triangulated (`(v0,v1,v2),(v0,v2,v3),…`), matching `three/examples/jsm/loaders/OBJLoader`'s own convention. A face referencing an out-of-range index is skipped, never thrown — same graceful-degradation rule as every other parser in this codebase.
  - **`src/plyParser.ts`** (`parsePly()`) — the genuinely harder of the two: PLY's ASCII **header** (element/property declarations) always precedes the body regardless of the body's own encoding (`ascii`, `binary_ little_endian`, or `binary_big_endian`, declared in the header's `format` line) — the header/body boundary is found by decoding the WHOLE buffer as `latin1` first (1 byte = 1 char, no multi-byte sequences, so a char index found via `indexOf("end_header")` is always identical to the real byte offset into the original buffer, safe regardless of whether the body itself is binary) and looking for the newline after `end_header`. Every declared `property` (including ones this codebase never reads — normals, colours, texture coordinates, confidence, …) is still correctly consumed by byte-width (binary) or token-count (ASCII) so the read cursor stays in sync for the NEXT record — only `vertex`'s `x`/`y`/`z` and `face`'s `vertex_indices`/ `vertex_index` (both spellings accepted; older exporters use the singular) are actually kept. Type widths cover both the modern (`int8`/`uint16`/`float32`/…) and legacy (`char`/`short`/`float`/…) PLY type-name spellings, since real files use either interchangeably. **Verified against real hand-built binary fixtures in both endiannesses** (`plyParser.test.ts` constructs actual `binary_little_endian`/`binary_big_endian` byte buffers via Node's `Buffer.writeFloatLE`/`writeFloatBE`, including EXTRA per-vertex properties declared after `x`/`y`/`z` specifically to prove the skip-unknown-properties byte-alignment logic works, not just the happy-path all-positions-only case) — not assumed correct from reading the spec alone. A truncated/malformed binary body degrades to whatever was successfully parsed before the cutoff (never throws); a genuinely unparseable file (no `end_header` at all) does throw a clear error, same "structurally not this format at all" convention `stlParser.ts`'s sibling scanners use elsewhere in this codebase.
  - **`src/objSolidSignatures.ts`/`src/plySolidSignatures.ts`** mirror `stlSolidSignatures.ts` exactly (same `connectedComponents`/ `boundsOfTriangles`/`boundsCenter`/`boundsDiagonal`/`volumeOfTriangles` calls from `meshComponents.ts`, zero new geometry code needed) — the ONLY difference from the STL version is skipping the `weldTriangleSoup()` step, since both formats already hand over shared-vertex indices.
  - **`modelDiffHost.ts`'s `CompareSource` union** gained `{kind:"obj", bytes}`/`{kind:"ply", bytes}` alongside the existing `"brep"`/`"stl"` — any side can be any kind, in any combination (verified end-to-end: OBJ-vs-PLY directly, and each against the STEP bull). **glTF was deferred at this point, then closed later as its own roadmap item — see the "glTF support for compare_models" section below.** The reasoning recorded here at the time still stands as an accurate description of the difficulty (accessor decoding across 5 component types with an optional `normalized` flag, sparse-accessor overlays, interleaved `bufferView` `byteStride` handling, and — the part with no OBJ/PLY analogue at all — full scene-graph traversal composing each node's TRS/matrix transform down to its mesh primitives); what changed is not the difficulty but the *validation story*, which was the actual blocker: "this codebase had no ready way to validate a hand-rolled implementation against the breadth of real-world glTF files/exporters." It turned out there was one.
  - **Mesh-format edits are NOT baked in for any of STL/OBJ/PLY** (there is no host-side mesh edit engine — same accepted limitation `generate_mesh`'s STL path already has) — `modelComparePanel.ts` and `compareModelsTool` both check for pending sidecar ops on a mesh-format side and surface a `⚠`/`warnings` entry (now naming the actual format, e.g. "OBJ sources have no host-side edit engine") rather than silently comparing stale-looking geometry with no explanation. `modelComparePanel.ts`'s file picker/gate (`COMPARE_FILTER`) now accepts `.obj`/`.ply` alongside STEP/IGES/BREP/STL.
- **Reuses existing OCCT helpers with zero new geometry code**: `occtOperations.ts`'s `bboxCenter` (already exported) and `bboxDiagonal` (module-private until this feature — now exported, since `modelDiffHost.ts` needed it and duplicating the identical `Bnd_Box`/ `CornerMin`/`CornerMax` logic would have been the actual oversight) are the same centroid/diagonal math `explodeSolids` already uses; volume comes from the same adaptive `BRepGProp` call shape (`src/brepGProp.ts`'s wrapper) `massProperties.ts`'s `solidProperties` uses. The absolute centroid-distance tolerance is `1e-3 × the larger model's whole-shape bboxDiagonal` — the same tolerance-fraction convention `gmshPartsMap.ts` already established for geometric bbox-center matching.
- **`modelComparePanel.ts` is the second standalone `vscode.window.createWebviewPanel` in this extension** (`whatsNew.ts` was the first) — `enableScripts: false`, plain HTML tables, no nonce/script needed since there's no interactivity beyond closing the tab. The `cad-preview.compareModels` command is registered standalone (like `cad-preview.open`/`whatsNew`, not gated behind `activeSession`, since it should work with no CAD Preview tab focused) but still reads `this.activeSession?.uri` as the default for model A, so comparing against "whatever I currently have open" needs only one file picker, not two.
- **Verified end-to-end against the real WASM via `npm run mcp:smoke`**, not just unit-tested: the smoke script diffs the edited fixture (`bull.stp` + an `addBox` op, 2 solids) against a fresh unedited copy of the same fixture (1 solid) and asserts exactly 1 matched (the bull, `centreDistance`/ `volumeDeltaPct` both ~0 — an exact self-match), 0 added, 1 removed (the box) — confirming the matching direction (`removed` = "only in A", the edited/first argument) is exactly as documented, not backwards. Every mesh format is verified against a real file too, not a synthetic fixture: `examples/STL/cube.stl` (a real 10×10×10 cube) against itself resolves the correct real volume (1000, exactly) and an exact self-match with zero WASM involvement on either side; `examples/OBJ/cube.obj` and `examples/PLY/ cube.ply` (both a real unit cube) each resolve volume 1 against themselves, and directly against each other (`formatA:"obj", formatB:"ply"`, confirming the mixed-mesh-source dispatch path, not just mesh-vs-B-rep); a STEP-vs-STL and a STEP-vs-OBJ cross-format call each confirm the mixed-source dispatch path (one side OCCT, one side a pure parser) works end-to-end; and a STEP-vs-glTF call confirms glTF still degrades to a clear `supported:false` message (listing the now-larger STEP/IGES/BREP/STL/OBJ/PLY supported set), not a crash, now that three of its former format-family siblings are supported.

## glTF support for `compare_models` (roadmap item, closed)

The last format gap in Compare Models. STL/OBJ/PLY each got a hand-rolled host-side parser; glTF was **deliberately** excluded (see the "Model comparison" section above), and the stated blocker was never the difficulty of the format — it was that "this codebase had no ready way to validate a hand-rolled implementation against the breadth of real-world glTF files/exporters." Shipping a plausible-but-subtly-wrong parser would produce confidently-wrong centroids/volumes, the exact misleading-false-match mode the feature was designed to avoid.

- **The blocker dissolved on a single empirical finding: three.js's own `GLTFLoader` runs headless under Node with a ONE-LINE polyfill.** `globalThis.ProgressEvent ??= class {}` is all it needs (its `onProgress` callback constructs one; this repo's vitest config has no jsdom). Verified on `examples/GLTF/cube.gltf` (8 verts / 12 tris) and on a two-node translate+rotate+scale variant (2 meshes / 24 tris). It fails ONLY on textures (`self is not defined` from the texture path), and deleting `materials`/`textures`/`images`/`samplers` — which cannot affect geometry — makes it work again. So the reference implementation, the very loader the webview already uses to DISPLAY these files, is usable as a permanent cross-validation oracle in unit tests. That is a materially stronger validation story than the fixtures OBJ/PLY were accepted on.
- **`src/gltfParser.ts`** (pure, vscode/OCCT/THREE-free) — `parseGltf(bytes, external?): WeldedMesh`, plus `listExternalBufferUris`/`resolveExternalBuffers`. Handles both containers (GLB detected by the `glTF` magic at offset 0, then 4-byte-aligned JSON/BIN chunks; otherwise UTF-8 JSON), all five component types, `normalized` (KHR_mesh_quantization), `bufferView.byteStride` interleaving, sparse accessors (including the legal no-base-bufferView all-zero case), primitive modes 4/5/6, and full scene-graph `matrix`-or-`T·R·S` composition with a mandatory cycle guard.
- **Every read goes through `DataView` with explicit little-endian, never a typed-array view over a bufferView offset.** glTF guarantees only 4-byte alignment relative to the *buffer*, and `Buffer` from `fs.readFileSync` is POOLED with an arbitrary `byteOffset` — a `Float32Array` constructed there throws outright. A unit fixture deliberately places the whole file at a non-4-aligned offset so this can't be "optimized" back later. (The same hazard bit a throwaway probe script during this work, from the other direction: `buf.buffer.slice(0)` slices the whole shared pool rather than the buffer's own bytes, silently yielding garbage geometry that varied run to run.)
- **Unlike `parseObj`/`parsePly`, this parser WELDS internally** and returns a `WeldedMesh`. Each primitive and each node instance is its own index space, so a single shared space is required before `connectedComponents()` can find anything. Exporters routinely split one watertight solid across primitives (one per material) with duplicated seam vertices — without welding, a per-material-split cube reports **six open sheets with no volume** instead of one closed solid. This also makes `meshHeal.ts` correct for free (glTF goes into `parseToWeldedMesh`'s already-indexed branch). Accepted, documented trade-off, identical to STL's: a "solid" is a connected component, so touching objects merge and material-split meshes join — the reported count can differ from a user's mental object count in both directions.
- **Compressed geometry is a DENY-list on `extensionsRequired`, and it throws** — `KHR_draco_mesh_compression`/`EXT_meshopt_compression` (plus `EXT_mesh_gpu_instancing` only when *required*). Deliberately not an allow-list, and deliberately not keyed off `extensionsUsed`: nearly every real file declares geometry-irrelevant `KHR_materials_*`/`KHR_texture_*` extensions an allow-list would wrongly reject. `KHR_mesh_quantization` is genuinely supported.
- **Two deliberate breaks with the sibling parsers' "malformed content degrades silently" rule**, both documented in the module: a denied compression extension, and an external `.bin` an accessor actually needs but wasn't supplied, both **throw** naming the cause. A silently-empty or half-empty mesh is precisely the confidently-wrong result this feature exists to avoid. The missing-buffer throw is unconditional, NOT gated on "produced no triangles" — a file whose other primitives read fine would otherwise return a partial mesh, which is just as wrong.
- **`resolveExternalBuffers` refuses any URI with a scheme, any absolute path, and any `..` that escapes the model's directory** (including URI-encoded `%2e%2e`), never calling the reader for them. A `.gltf` is untrusted input and this extension is read-only by design. The parser itself stays fs-free; `mcpTools.ts` passes a `node:fs` reader, `provider.ts`/`modelComparePanel.ts` a `vscode.workspace.fs` one.
- **Validation is two files, deliberately split.** `gltfParser.test.ts` (52 cases) is framework-free and fast, with a `buildDoc` helper that emits every fixture in BOTH containers via `it.each(["gltf","glb"])` — which makes GLB coverage nearly free. `gltfParser.crossvalidation.test.ts` (32 cases) quarantines everything that touches `three` + the DOM polyfill, announced in the filename; precedent exists both ways in-repo (`meshLoaders.test.ts` imports three; `meshExporters.test.ts` polyfills a browser global at the top with a comment). The oracle compares only **winding- and order-invariant** quantities — exact triangle count, a multiset of triangles keyed by quantized positions sorted WITHIN each triangle, volume/area to 1e-4, bounds to 1e-5, exact component count — because three legitimately differs: it does NOT flip winding for a mirrored node (it flips `material.side` at render time instead). Winding is asserted only in the pure test.
- **The oracle was verified to actually discriminate, not just to agree.** A deliberate one-line bug (ignoring `bufferView.byteStride`) was injected and the suite failed on exactly the interleaved fixture in both containers, then reverted. `expectMatchesOracle` also asserts the fixture produced non-zero geometry — without that, two parsers that both silently produced nothing would "agree" perfectly.
- **The constant duplication was consolidated, reversing a documented decision.** `COMPARABLE_MESH_FORMATS` lived in BOTH `mcpTools.ts` and `modelComparePanel.ts`, justified by a comment saying "the two files don't otherwise share an import" — factually stale, since both already import `routeFile` from `fileRouter.ts`. It now lives in `fileRouter.ts` alongside a new `MeshParseFormat` type. This mattered concretely: adding glTF meant widening **nine** `as "stl" | "obj" | "ply"` casts, **two of which were runtime string comparisons** (`provider.ts`'s `meshHealRequest` gate and `handlePromoteToBrep`) that TypeScript would NOT have flagged — they'd have left the Mesh Health panel and the Promote button silently broken for glTF while every test passed.
- **A triangle-count ceiling shipped with this, not as a follow-up.** `meshHeal.ts`'s `MAX_HEALABLE_TRIANGLES` (50 000) makes `checkMeshHealth`/`promoteMeshToBrep` refuse an oversized mesh with an actionable error. Both build **one `BRepBuilderAPI_MakeFace` per triangle** and sew them, so cost scales directly with triangle count and every face is an Emscripten heap handle. This repo's own mesh fixtures are 12 triangles, but glTF is a *rendering* interchange format whose real-world files routinely carry 100k–1M — the risk existed for STL already, but glTF makes hitting it near-certain. An honest "too large" beats an opaque out-of-memory abort.
- **Verified end-to-end against the live WASM, not just unit-tested** (`npm run mcp:smoke`): `cube.gltf` and the new `cube.glb` self-match exactly and match EACH OTHER exactly (volume 1, centreDistance 0 — proving both containers decode identically); `two-boxes.gltf` resolves to exactly 2 solids at x=±5 (the node-transform guard — ignoring transforms would collapse it to 1); glTF-vs-PLY and STEP-vs-glTF cross-format both work; `check_mesh_health` closes both containers at the tightest 1e-6 rung with healed volume exactly 1; and `promote_mesh_to_brep` on `cube.glb` → STEP is re-verified through a **separate** `load_model` + `get_mass_properties` pair (1 solid, volume 1) rather than merely checking the promote call didn't throw.
- **New committed fixtures**: `examples/GLTF/cube.glb` (the repo had no binary GLB anywhere — roughly half of real-world glTF usage had zero coverage) and `examples/GLTF/two-boxes.gltf` (one mesh instanced by two translated nodes).

## 2D technical drawings — triangle-based hidden-line removal (roadmap Tier 1, closed)

`export_technical_drawing` draws feature edges with occluded runs dashed, for B-rep AND mesh sources. **It un-blocks a Non-goal this file recorded as kernel-blocked, without contradicting a word of that finding**: `HLRBRep_*` is still unavailable and `HLRAppli_ReflectLines` is still the wrong tool — this calls no B-rep HLR API at all, working on the tessellated triangles already on hand.

- **Exact, not sampled — and that removed a whole parameter.** Under orthographic projection both an edge's depth and an occluding triangle's plane-depth are **affine in the edge parameter `t`**, so their difference has at most one root, solvable in closed form: clip the projected edge to the triangle's three half-planes, evaluate the margin at both ends, solve the single crossing. No sample spacing to tune, no thin occluder stepped over, no run boundary quantized to a sample. `basis.forward` was already computed and never used by `project()` — the depth scalar was sitting there free.
- **Self-occlusion: exclusion is exact, but the epsilon is what actually carries it — measured, not assumed.** Every feature edge lies ON the surface, so its two adjacent triangles interpolate to the same depth along it. Excluding them is provably safe (a planar triangle's interior lies strictly on one side of the line through any of its own edges, so it can never strictly contain a point of that edge). But **bug injection showed removing the exclusion changes no test** until `depthEpsilon` is set to 0: the shared-edge margin is exactly `0`, and `0 < -epsilon` is already false. The exclusion is kept — free, skips two triangles per edge — but the comment now says which one is load-bearing, and a test pins it at `depthEpsilon: 0`.
- **Projection is CENTRED on the model bbox first, and that is not cosmetic.** `positions` is `Float32Array`, whose relative precision is 2^-24 of the *coordinate magnitude*, not of the model's size. A 10 mm part at world x = 10000 — routine in a STEP assembly — carries ~1e-4 of its own extent in representation error, which swamps real depth margins and produces speckled visible/hidden noise that reads as a hatching style rather than a bug.
- **For a B-rep, a crease is a cross-FACE edge, not an angle.** `weldedMeshFromTessellation` now carries a per-triangle face-id array through the weld (welding maps vertices but preserves triangle order, so a parallel array stays aligned). This is exact and density-independent. The angle path exists only for mesh sources, which have no face identity — default 35°, chosen against real exporters (a 12-segment STL cylinder has 30° facets; typical exports are 5–15°), and deliberately well above `meshFacets.ts`'s 15° coplanarity tolerance, which answers a different question and would produce a wireframe here.
- **The crease-explosion warning is gated on size AND the absence of face ids, because the obvious heuristic cries wolf on the most ordinary shape there is.** A cube legitimately has 12 of its 18 interior edges as creases — 67%, past any sane fraction. Simple polyhedra are *supposed* to be almost all crease, so the check only applies above 64 interior edges, and never to a B-rep (face identity cannot explode).
- **A real defect that every count passed and only LOOKING caught.** An SVG subpath restarts `stroke-dasharray` at zero, so a hidden segment shorter than one dash renders fully "on" — and a tessellated hidden curve (a hole rim is dozens of short segments) drew **solid**, indistinguishable from a visible edge. `segmentCount`/`hiddenSegmentCount` were both correct throughout; the drawing simply lied. Fixed by chaining hidden runs into polylines before emitting, reusing `segmentsToPolylines` — which had to **move from `dxfSilhouette.ts` into `svgSilhouette.ts`** (re-exported for compatibility) because the DXF writer already imports the SVG one and the reverse would be a cycle. Pinned by a regression test asserting one `M` and three `L`s for a three-segment chain.
- **DXF chains visible and hidden SEPARATELY**, which is required rather than tidy: `segmentsToPolylines` joins by exact endpoint match, so one concatenated pass would chain a visible run straight into the hidden run it meets and emit a single polyline that is half a lie. Hidden geometry lands on a `HIDDEN` **layer** — the mechanism `DIMENSIONS` already uses — explicitly not a dashed linetype, which would need an `LTYPE`/`LAYER` table this writer does not emit at all.
- **`export_technical_drawing` is a thin wrapper over `exportSvgSilhouetteTool`, not a parallel handler** — the gate, view/unit resolution, annotation read, source construction, path guard and write are identical, and duplicating that chain would be a second place for the view convention to drift. While wiring it, the shared handler's view lookup moved from the curated `SVG_VIEWS` to `resolveNamedView` (a strict superset), so both tools now accept all 14 named views and the QuickPick still shows 7.
- **Verified**: `hiddenLineRemoval.test.ts` (15 cases) — the isometric cube (12 edges, 9 visible, 3 hidden), asserted as *whole* edges rather than counts alone, since a drawing fragmented into slivers still totals correctly; a partial-occlusion fixture splitting one edge at exactly the occluder's boundary, which the cube cannot catch because every cube edge is all-or-nothing; coplanar geometry hiding nothing; and degenerate/NaN input. Bug injection confirmed a flipped depth comparison and a removed self-occlusion guard both fail. Live `mcp:smoke` reproduces the same 12/9/3 through real OCCT, and the holed-plate drawing was rasterized and **looked at** — which is the only reason the dash defect above was found.
- **A test-premise error worth recording**: an early identity assertion checked that the three hidden edges share a point. They do — but so do the three *near* edges, so a fully inverted depth test passed it. Worse, in isometric the near and far corners differ by `(1,1,1)`, parallel to the view, so they project to the very same screen point and "which corner" cannot discriminate either. The working assertion is on the far corner's *neighbours*.

## SVG silhouette export (roadmap item, closed)

The roadmap listed this as **"blocked pending a probe"** of `HLRAppli_ReflectLines` — the one green survivor after every `HLRBRep_*` class turned out red. It shipped, but **not** on that kernel path.

- **`HLRAppli_ReflectLines` was probed against the live WASM and rejected on the drawing, not on the binding.** The binding works: the constructor is **unsuffixed** (no `_1`/`_2` variants exist), and `SetAxes(Nx,Ny,Nz, atX,atY,atZ, upX,upY,upZ)` → `Perform()` → `GetResult()` are all bound and functional — 249 ms on `bull.stp`, returning a non-null compound that this codebase's own `enumerateEdges` reads as 25 edges. It was rejected because **the resulting drawing is worse, which is only visible by looking at it**: rendered side by side against the tessellation silhouette of the same view, `GetResult()` gave the outer boundary and a few fragments while missing the part's circular holes and interior cutout entirely, where the tessellation path drew all of them. `GetResult()` returns reflect lines only; sharp feature edges live behind `GetCompoundOf3dEdges(type, visible, in3d)`, whose `type` is an `HLRBRep_TypeOfResultingEdge` from the entirely-red family — calling it throws (`Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'value')`). The one filter that would make the kernel path competitive is unreachable. **This is the "green in the manifest is necessary but not sufficient" pattern for the third time in this codebase** (after `Message_ProgressRange_1` and `Interface_Static.CVal`), with a new wrinkle: here the binding itself was fine and only the *usefulness* of its output failed, which no amount of API probing would have revealed — it needed rendering the output and inspecting it.
- **Shipped on triangle adjacency instead, which is strictly more capable here.** `src/silhouetteEdges.ts`'s `silhouetteEdges(positions, indices, direction)` keeps an edge when its two adjacent triangles disagree about facing, **or** it has exactly one adjacent triangle (an open boundary — this is what lets an unclosed STL/glTF still draw as something), **or** 3+ (non-manifold, always a real feature — the same judgement `meshTopology.ts` applies). A triangle exactly edge-on (`dot === 0`) counts as back-facing: deterministic, and it puts exactly one line at a cylinder's tangent instead of zero or two. Works for B-rep (via `tessellateByGroup`) **and** STL/OBJ/PLY/glTF, which `HLRAppli_ReflectLines` could never have handled, and needs no WASM at all for the mesh formats — the "opening a pure-mesh file must never load the WASM" invariant holds.
- **Welding is REQUIRED on the B-rep path, not an optimization.** `tessellateByGroup` returns one independent index space per face, so without `weldTriangleSoup` every face boundary reads as an open-boundary edge and the "silhouette" degenerates into a full wireframe of every face. Also note `tessellateByGroup` returns **raw typed arrays** (`GeometryBuffers`), not the base64 `EncodedMesh` the protocol uses — the base64 encoding happens later in `loadBRep`.
- **`edgeKey` was consolidated into `meshComponents.ts`** and exported; `meshTopology.ts` now imports it, and `silhouetteEdges.ts` would have been its FOURTH verbatim copy. Same drift-hazard removal as the shared `enumerateEdges`/`EDGE_DEFLECTION` work on the B-rep side.
- **`src/svgSilhouette.ts` is this codebase's first runtime SVG WRITER** (`svgImport.ts` only reads; `toolbarIcons.ts`/`opIcons.ts` embed markup generated offline by the TikZ pipeline). Output is deliberately minimal — one `<path>`, no `<style>`, no script, no external references. **1 SVG user unit = 1 model unit** with physical `width`/`height` in mm, so a native-mm drawing prints 1:1 in any vector tool. Y is negated (SVG grows downward — the same flip `svgImport.ts` applies inbound). The `up` hint falls back to `[0,0,1]` when (anti-)parallel to the view direction, the gimbal case a straight-down TOP view hits.
- **Two real precision defects were found and fixed by verification, not by review.** (1) The stroke width was going through the same fixed-4-decimal formatter as coordinates, so on a small model — or the same model converted to inches or feet — it rounded toward and eventually to **zero**, and a `stroke-width="0"` path renders as nothing at all; it now uses significant digits (`toPrecision(6)`), with a guard, since there is exactly one per document. (2) Coordinate precision is now **adaptive to the drawing's extent** (`decimalsFor`, ~6 significant figures) rather than fixed: a 10 mm cube exported in FEET has an extent of ~0.034, which fixed 4-decimal coordinates would quantize to barely two significant digits and visibly distort. The first surfaced as a unit test asserting a stroke-width ratio; the second as an `mcp:smoke` assertion that a `unit:"in"` export scales by exactly 1/25.4 (it came out 25.403).
- **Degenerate input always yields a VALID document**, never `NaN`/`Infinity` in the viewBox: zero segments, or everything projecting to a single point, emits a well-formed empty/unit-box `<svg>`. Non-finite coordinates skip their segment; `polylinesSvg` breaks the chain around one rather than bridging it.
- **A closed loop through this repo's OWN SVG reader is the strongest cheap check on the `d` syntax** — `svgSilhouette.test.ts` feeds the writer's output to `svgImport.ts`'s `parseSvgPaths()` (the Import SVG feature's parser) and asserts it recovers exactly the segments that went in, coordinates included.
- **`src/svgSilhouetteHost.ts` reuses `CompareSource`** (from `modelDiffHost.ts`) rather than declaring a near-identical union: it already means "a model to read geometry from", and its `brep` variant carries `ops`, which this needs so the drawing reflects the EDITED model. It also keeps both features' format support in lockstep automatically. (It does couple two features through one type — a deliberate call, flagged rather than hidden.) Defaults to `quality: "fine"`, not `"standard"`: the silhouette's smoothness IS the tessellation resolution, with no shading to hide faceting. Short MEMFS path (`/sv.<format>`), `wrapOcctFault` on the catch, handles freed in reverse in `finally`.
- **`SVG_VIEWS` lives in the pure `svgSilhouette.ts`, NOT in `mcpTools.ts`** — `provider.ts` needs it too, and importing it from the MCP layer would drag that whole module into the extension bundle.
- **`"svg"` is deliberately NOT a `CadFormat`.** Beyond rippling through `EXPORT_EXTENSION`/`EXPORT_LABEL`/`exportTargetsFor`/`fileRouter.ts`, it would land in `package.json`'s `customEditors.selector` and make VS Code try to **open** `.svg` files in the 3D viewer, colliding head-on with the existing Import SVG… flow. `handleScreenshot`'s PNG is the established precedent for an output format that isn't a `CadFormat`, and `handleExportSvg` follows `handlePromoteToBrep`'s structure (quick-picks → the shared `promptSaveAndWrite`).
- **"Current view" needs no new protocol message** — `provider.ts` already tracks `currentViewState` (updated from the `viewChanged` handler for the `.view.json` sidecar), and `ViewState` carries `viewDirection` + `cameraUp`. It is the first entry in the view quick-pick when a view state exists.
